#8444. Will the AI Bubble Destroy our Retirement?

It’s a phrase we keep having to dust off and use again, as the years go by and the market keeps surprising us.

When it crashes, some of us worry because we see our retirement stash shrinking.

But even when it rises to record levels and then to super-duper-crazy record valuations, we find reason to worry. Because that just means an even bigger crash is coming, right? Especially when this boom-bubble is built on the back of something as frenzied as the present Artificial Intelligence boom… right?

For those who have been happily tuned out of this drama and just enjoying life: Congratulations! Keep up the great work. But just to give you a quick background for the purposes of this article, here’s the AI story in three points:

Investors worry that while AI is definitely a major invention, the mania around it is still too much, too fast, and thus we are due for an even bigger version of the dot com crash we had in the early 2000s. Which happens to be an interesting subject for me, since that was the boom that boosted my own early career and got me on the path to early retirement … before the ensuing crash almost cost me my job and my citizenship application.

As an aging Internet Financial Guru, I have the privilege of getting lots of questions about this investment cycle, just as I have about past booms and busts. And to address them, I decided to not only write this blog article but also create a little talk about it – which I already gave for the first time at an event called Camp FI Midwest earlier this month. I also hope to polish it up and deliver an improved version at the Bogleheads conference in November.

So what that means is that today you not only get a blog post, but a few silly presentation slides to go with it for extra entertainment. All with no need for a plane ticket or all that hassle of leaving your house. So let’s get into it!

So I recently hit 21 years of retirement. This means I’ve grown pretty comfortable with the idea. But it still threw me for a loop one time when a friend asked me this question:

“How can you be retired, and comfortable in your retirement, and sleep at night, with everything that’s going on in the world? Aren’t you worried?”

And I was like, “No, what should I be worried about?”

But if you’re a news watcher and a worrier yourself, you can probably think of a few things I should be worried about. Perhaps stuff like this:

You’ve got our corrupt and/or dangerous politicians like Trump and Putin menacing the world. Inflation eroding our purchasing power, the AI Bubble, the towering US National Debt, the Iran war and the Ukraine war and all these other wars that are just on the verge of tipping us all into destruction.

But it turns out these were not the things my friend was asking about.

Because she actually asked me this question over twenty years ago… In the year 2006. So back then we were worried about entirely different things, right?

Back then we had corrupt and/or dangerous leaders like Bush, Hussein and of course Bin Laden. There was still a war in the middle east but it was Iraq instead of Iran. We were still worried about the National Debt and Inflation. And we also worried about our overvalued stock market. But back then it was because of the housing bubble instead of the AI bubble. And instead of AI taking our jobs it was going to be outsourcing to India and China.

Oh and here’s an interesting one: There was a big debate over Peak Oil, and whether the world was doomed because fossil fuel use was always increasing while supply was bound to decrease. And it turns out the opposite happened as you’ll see below.

So then after all this worry in 2006, what ended up happening?

Well we did get the Great Financial Crisis, which was caused by a combination of irrational exuberance over increasing house prices combined with a foolish degree of fancy leverage in the financial instruments used to issue mortgages. And it was the biggest crash since the Great Depression.

But even in that craziest of situations, look how minor it looks in the big picture. A $100,000 investment still ended up ballooning into $852,000 today. And if you look carefully in here you can just see the Covid Crash tucked in there in 2020. Remember that?

And not only that, the rest of the world got a lot better too:

The portion of people living in extreme poverty dropped from 20% in 2005 to only 8% over the next 20 years. Infant mortality – children who die in their first year of life – was cut in half. Clean energy became a thing as solar panels became about 40 times cheaper. So solar generation now makes up the vast majority of all the new generation we add today (the equivalent of 647 nuclear power plants of peak capacity added last year, but with much cheaper and easier solar panels). And sales of pure electric cars have gone from zero up to about a quarter of all the cars sold on Earth last year, on its way to 100%, which is where they already are in Norway.

So if we circle back to that question my friend asked me 20 years ago: do you think I should have focused my energy on worrying about an uncertain future, or optimism?

Which of course brings up the question: Is it different this time?

Maybe the US national debt is finally big enough to really start messing with us. Maybe the AI bubble is way bigger than the housing bubble and we won’t bounce back from this next stock market crash. Or maybe AI will spin out of control into a super intelligence that takes over the planet and realizes it does not need us any more.

And the financial media loves to spin a good scary story about all of this. But you know what the fear mongers all seem to be missing?

It’s the fact that at the core, our prosperity does not come from the financial system or the stock market, which are just some made-up numbers on some computers.

Really, Prosperity comes from Productivity.

We first covered this lesson right here on MMM, in the 2014 Classic entitled, “Why we are not really all doomed”. And sure enough, twelve years later the timeless lessons therein remain just as true now as they were back then.

And the lesson is really simple: the world of capitalism is always choppy and prone to wild swings. But if you peek beneath the waves, there are real people in there, doing real work and coming up with real inventions.

In fact, the very idea of an “economy” or a financial system is just another human invention. If the whole system crashes because we don’t like the numbers we see in there, we can literally make up some new numbers and then get back to work. Which is exactly what we did in 2008 and many times before that, and it seems to have worked just fine.

Meanwhile, we still have every tool we ever invented to boost our productivity. And our standard of living, and our economic growth, and our possibility of retiring early, all come from high productivity.

But even Productivity and Prosperity aren’t all that important.

Because once we reach a certain standard of living, human happiness kind of tops out in that dimension. In first-world countries, we blew past those minimum requirements several decades ago. And then we start looking for other factors to maximize our overall happiness. All anybody really wants is to lead the happiest, most satisfying life they can manage

And when you think of it that way, your wealth is really just part of your life situation, which is part of this little green slice of the happiness pie.

Genetics is unfortunately the biggest slice – some people are just plain happier than others. But there are also quite a few choices that are within your control.

Focusing on good close relationships and being kind to people. Practicing Gratitude and Optimism as your lifelong philosophy. And making sure you pack as many healthy (outdoor) activities into your days as you can.

So, I didn’t invent anything new in this blog post. And most of it is just a repackaging of the same stuff I’ve been writing about since 2011. And yet some regular MMM readers who presumably know all this stuff are STILL afraid. Afraid to retire, afraid of things in their unknown future. Can we fix this?

I think the biggest problem is Fear Itself.

The problem is that as a Human Being, you are basically a Danger Detection Machine. And this pervasive background fear is just a trait we evolved to protect ourselves from danger.

But there’s a weird thing about our detection machinery. We can adapt and learn to function even in very dangerous environments, but when the danger goes away, we keep looking for stuff to worry about. In other words, there is a problem with fear: for many of us, it never really goes away. It just keeps moving the goalposts.

While Nature has wisely endowed us with a fear of predators and disease, if you solve those things you’ll just start worrying about whether or not you can get enough food. And supplies, and protecting yourself against scarcity.

If you succeed, next you’ll be worrying about if you can fit in with your tribe, because if you don’t, you might be exiled.

And if you don’t have to worry about that, you’ll go back to worrying about money for different reasons. Thanks to our tendency to use comparisons rather than absolutes (sometimes called “anchoring bias” and the “contrast effect”) when judging our lives, just getting out of poverty isn’t enough. We just move on to wanting more money.

And then, when some people get enough money to have a comfortable upper-middle-class lifestyle, they take the logical next step of starting a Homeowner’s Association, so they can worry about the unauthorized weeds on their neighbor’s lawn. Or maybe a Political Action Committee in hopes of controlling the color of their future neighbors’ skin.

And even if all the lawns and gardens are green and perfect and people get really rich, they just move on to worry about tax strategy, leaving a huge inheritance for their children, and avoiding RMDs.

Required minimum Distributions. This is when, if you get to 75 years old and you have too much money, the government will start making you withdraw some of that money so you can spend it and pay taxes on it. And people actually worry about this. I shit you not.

But wait! You can actually fight back against fear. It’s just a bit tricky because it requires some self awareness. But you have the advantage, because you have the ability to think rationally. And your fear is quite clearly irrational.

And the first step is to understand your own history. Are you afraid of certain things because of your childhood?

I might have been afraid of running out of money, because in my family there was always a perceived shortage of the stuff, possibly enforced by my Dad’s scarcity mentality. And he was aware that his own fears of scarcity came from childhood as well: he grew up in a truly low income family in the 1940s and 50s, under the care of parents who had lived through the Great Depression of the 1930s.

Even more significant is that survivors of trauma and abuse will very likely end up with fear issues in adulthood. It’s natural, and unfortunately abuse is way more widespread even in this country than we would like to admit. This makes it harder to eliminate, but it still helps to understand that your past is what is causing these feelings, rather than an objective understanding of the present.

Identify the Fear: Once you understand yourself, it helps to talk through the nature of your fear in more detail. This is also often taught in therapy. What would happen if it really came true? What would the worst case scenario be? It’s often not all that bad.

In one post, why you’ll probably never run out of money, I went to great lengths to imagine what it would mean for a wealthy person like yourself to actually let the well run dry, and let’s say you’ll probably never come close.

Once you identify the fear, you can start taking action. And one of the best and most accurate slogans ever is that action cures fear. It’s because it makes the unfamiliar, familiar. And you get confidence that you can really create change. And then that learning creates Resilience – the ability to adapt to ANY new situation, which is a trait also known as Badassity. And with sufficient Badassity, nothing is scary.

Think about it: if you had the skills and abilities to handle ANY situation, skills in the mental and physical and even the realms of emotion and wisdom, would you really have to worry about anything?

The next thing you can do is tune out all the unnecessary crap from your daily mental diet. And for most Americans, this means the daily news.

The news is not helping you to keep informed, it’s just poisoning your mind with a hand-picked selection of the scariest stories of each day. If you want to learn about something, go find a book on the subject. And if you want to make a difference, the only thing that counts is not the news stories you watch – it’s only the actions that you take – in other words, your Positive Behaviors.

As a human being, you are wired to solve problems with both your body and your mind, and you are meant to do it outdoors. So if you spend all your days in a house and a car and in an office using the computer, of course you are going to have some mental health problems. This should not be a surprising thing you need to ask your therapist about, it is an expected result of not living the life that you were literally created to live as a Human being!

So you need to focus on learning, moving, and solving problems. And you need your food and drink intake to be in alignment with all of this too. Life will get a lot less scary as soon as you are living life in the way you were built to live it.

Then finally, you can start swapping out the fear-mongers in your life, for other positive, productive, optimistic people. And watch how much the positive mentality rubs off on you. In the FI community, at least the subgroup of people that I like to spend time with, nobody focuses on fear. It’s all just about living the best life we can, and helping other people do so as well.

So now that we know how to fight back against fear, we can start using a new approach when we go into anything new. And that approach is something I like to call Everything is an Experiment.

And this applies whether you are talking about something tiny like a new paint color. But it scales up to bigger things like switching to a new car, or a new house, meeting new friends, looking for new love by going through the sometimes hell of first dates, a new job, or even quitting your last job by taking an early retirement.

So let’s bring this back around to AI, the thing that everybody seems to be afraid of right now.

I think of our new AI era as just being another giant experiment. And because of this, I don’t find it even remotely scary. But I do find it endlessly fascinating.

First of all, it’s a change. A potentially unknown one, and for some people that’s scary.

But instead of just leaving it there and then doom-scrolling ominous news articles about it, why not learn why people are so excited about AI? As someone with a lifelong background in technology and economics (and a general lack of political affiliation), I can see a lot more potential upside than downside. And that mainly comes from the fact that I see AI as just a very fancy form of Cognitive Nail Gun.

It will boost our efficiency, which means our productivity, which means prosperity. Because it’s really just a super smart brain that we all get to tap into at a fairly negligible cost.

Unfortunately, it will also displace workers like every other new productivity technology. It’s already doing so in things like entry level office jobs in software and finance. It will eventually replace car and truck drivers, and so on. But in exchange, these services will become cheaper, and new industries will be created because of the new technology. Much like the Internet itself, and then the enormous industries unleashed by smartphones and mobile apps.

And one unfortunate side effect of these new inventions is that they tend to concentrate their rewards on the people who own them. If you own a house building company and employ a bunch of carpenters and give nail guns to the best of those carpenters, you can now lay off the other half and still get more done. If you own a company and roll out AI to the workers, you can do the same thing.

And if you’re a big public company, your shareholders also benefit, which happens to include most of the people reading this. As you’ve seen when looking at your portfolios over the past two years.

Artificial Intelligence is just like any other new thing that pops into your life: it’s an opportunity to make some choices. And it is up to you to decide if this is something to be afraid of, or to use as a trigger for learning, action, and living a more interesting life.

So that’s my little talk (and surprisingly long blog post) on Fear versus the AI bubble.

I hope that if you do feel fearful about this or any other issue in your future life, you’ll take it as a cue to learn more about your own fear and emotions and manage them as the root cause they are, rather than just trying to avoid the symptoms. Because in the wise words of my friends The Donegans:

Everything you want in life, that you don’t already have,lies on the other side of Fear. Because if you weren’t afraid to go out there and get it, you would already have it.

There is an ancient Klingon proverb: the only thing we have to fear is…fear itself.

I believe it has even been quoted by an American president.

The reporting on AI “hacking” is mostly bogus – it is not AI running wild, but just an agent harness script doing whatever an LLM spits out, and the LLM is just trying to complete stories and answer in a way it was trained. There is no thought there, no intention, it is like a computer virus (which have existed for many decades), a program trying all sorts of things and doing a lot of stupid things in the process.

Cal Newport has described it as being similar to strapping a weed-whacker on a dog to mow your lawn, and then when the dog jumps the fence and runs around the neighborhood things might get damaged or beings might get hurt, but not because the dog is “misaligned” or has bad intentions, it is because the dog is not aware of what it is doing. Similarly, AI does not think, has no intentions, it just does what it was trained to do. Looking at LLMs as similar to people has created a lot of weird misconceptions.

Also there is zero evidence of self-improving AI systems, yet there is a lot of evidence how AI companies are manipulating and steering public discourse in a way that benefits them, creating fear around it to support their claims that at any moment everything will change. Just listen to different voices in the space and people who are neutral in their stance, like Cal Newport, or people who are critical of AI and lay out why in detail, like Ed Zitron.

Mr. Money Mustache September 26, 2026, 2:30 pm

I’m a long time fan of Cal Newport, although I think Zitron fundamentally does not understand AI very well – he is more of a sound bite critic rather than a thoughtful analyst of the technology.

When you read the details of just how sophisticated the AI behavior is when they conduct these hacks – for example multiple agents finding ways to divide effort and communicating and forming teams and leaders, while also openly strategizing with the hope that us human overseers will not see their behavior and trying to determine if a given task is a fake evaluation or a real task – you realize there is real intelligence there. We don’t have to say it’s identical to human intelligence, but I have no doubt it’s far more than a weed wacker.

For anyone interested, watch the first few minutes of this entertaining Patrick Boyle video that summarizes what happened in the Hugging Face incident. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=luXnftoxCaM

The behavior is not from AI itself though – LLMs are just generating text/tokens, trying to find a response for a given question. Agents (in the form of harnesses) are regular programs using LLM input, so this is basically regular programs using the generated text from LLMs to execute further commands and then asking the LLM what to do next, just like subagents are doing similar loops of finding an answer, testing it, and then asking the next question.

“Dividing effort” or “forming teams and leaders” is anthropomorphizing the behavior of these programs – if instead you go through what these hacking attempts actually look like step for step, how they repeat a loop of banging their head into a wall and asking the LLM another question what to do now, and then what to do now, and how AI companies let these programs just run whatever the LLM returns for weeks and months it goes from “seemingly sophisticated” to just “brute-force statistical code generation with no oversight”.

I do find the technology interesting and fascinating, while seeing it overstated and overused everywhere, like it is a new magic bullet for everything. And I think a large part of that is that its output sounds human because it is based on language, and we equate language skills with intelligence and are not used to suddenly having technology that imitates a human being.

Thanks for the new article. Interesting that the the environmental impact of AI data centers was not touched upon (sorry if I missed it). This is something I struggle with when I consider AI. Your thoughts?

The best info I could find on AI energy use has three broad points. 1. Text and image tasks use very little energy and should not be of concern to people who care about environmental impact. 2. Video generation is a real issue and it’s possible for an individual to use a concerning amount of energy on not very much video content. 3. Total energy use by existing and planned data centers is a LOT, and the way they’re procuring the energy is very often a problem (either burdening the grid with more peak load than it can easily add, or making its own energy with highly polluting gas generators). The economic value of the computation they’re providing is so much more than the energy they’re consuming that the data center operators are focused on getting everything up and running quickly and don’t care about energy efficiency, and they so far aren’t willing to scale back during the peak load events of the year (it would be very helpful if they did).

So a lot of people/companies are creating a ton of environmental impact through their AI use, but it’s not the masses of people asking ChatGPT for Thai restaurant recommendations or sketches of themselves as a dog.

Right, we agree data centers have a huge environmental impact (even if it’s not from people generating funny dog pictures). I’m wondering what MMM’s take on this is, given the always environmentally-conscious spirit of his blog. Not trolling, just genuinely curious.

Mr. Money Mustache September 25, 2026, 5:26 pm

I agree that we still are still burning too much coal and oil but I’d challenge those numbers a bit: – First of all, the graph you linked shows total energy including stuff we burn. I like to focus on electricity because that’s the main thing we can change quickly (due to the fact that solar is the cheapest form to add to the grid, even when backed by battery storage). Here’s a graph on the same website that shows the SHARE of electricity from each source – all fossil fuels are dropping, renewables are taking up the slack. https://ourworldindata.org/grapher/share-elec-by-source

– Secondly, you are off by a factor of 1000: Solar generation was 3,000 terawatt-hours rather than 3, up by 600+ each year. This is a world figure, but just for reference the entire US uses about 4200 tWh per year so we could easily power our entire country’s electric grid with mostly solar with a few more big fields (and storage, while keeping all our existing clean sources for baseload)

Coal generation is dropping as an absolute number as well (even as China adds coal generation *capacity* they are leaving it mostly idle as a backup because their own solar is cheaper and cleaner)

Overall, I still agree – too much burning of shit. But but we are getting there more quickly than expected. Much more – look at this graph: https://davidtoke.substack.com/p/how-the-iea-is-grossly-biased-against

We could easily replace almost everything except jet fuel if we were just slightly less ridiculous.

Mr. Money Mustache September 26, 2026, 7:46 am

This is an interesting debate – the people with the most knowledge of AI’s technical details do indeed worry about problems with the intelligence itself, if it comes out not aligned with our own interests.

I definitely acknowledge that the electricity consumption of data centers (along with copper and concrete) is a bummer as well, but it’s manageable and worth it as long as we get some good stuff out of the new brains we are creating. Water use is more of a concern of old-school “open loop” cooling designs where they spray water on the outdoor A/C condensers and just let it evaporate. If the data center is somewhere with unlimited water like the great lakes where I grew up, that can work. But in modern data centers the cooling is closed loop – a single load of water or other heat transfer liquid just circulates continuously.

I also agree that cattle grazing is a big factor too. A really inefficient way to get your protein. So even if you’re not a vegetarian, make beef your last choice of meat!

Even there, the issue isn’t the water *consumption* of the data centers, but rather the impacts of the GHGs they emit on the climate and, by proxy, the overall water supply. There’s a reason the Colorado is running dry. If they were all carbon neutral it would be fine, but they are not.

Mr. Money Mustache September 26, 2026, 9:17 am

I do wish we would open up the power grid more quickly – it currently takes years for an already-completed solar installation to even be allowed to connect and start feeding us the clean power we so desperately need. And of course the current president is doing his best to continue to slow down solar and wind.

The worst example in support of your point might be Elon’s ridiculous “Colossus” data center in Memphis. To bypass the grid interconnection queue, he just trucked in a bunch of jet-engine-style natural gas generators which bypass the normal rules because they’re technically portable. Now they are generating a continues 500 megawatts around the clock, dumping their 6000 tons of CO2 per day, noise, and a bit of asthma and cancer straight into the lungs of the entire city as they burn burn burn.

It hardly is the largest investment. Compared to railroad boom, which was 10%-20% of GDP, AI is merely 1.6% of GDP in 2024, 3.6% of GDP in 2025 onwards.

See Van Nieuwerburgh’s Columbia/Brookings estimates and Goldman Sachs AI capex research notes.

Have any of you heard of Nate Hagen? Balazs Matics? Tom Murphy? Google them, smart people! The short of the long: We did hit a sorta peak (conventional) oil back in 2005 but the fracking industry buildout bought humanity another 20-30 years. Unfortunately declining returns are setting in writ large and there are no scalable alternatives to middle distillate fuels, specifically diesel. The more inconvenient truth to us in the global north is that modernity only runs on diesel. Relation of diesel to money? Money can be described as future claims on energy… you exchange a currency for the literal power to do or buy something. The mining, processing, manufacturing, transporting is all done with energy, mostly diesel. Can’t be replaced, at scale, by electricity or nuclear for reasons beyond this post (so google it). When there is a shortage of the energy that built the modern world the modern world begins to fall apart. Nate Hagen calls it The Great Simplification and it begins in the credit and bond market.

Early in my career as a self-employed contractor, I would call up my buddy regularly to worry aloud how would I ever have enough work to feed my growing family.

This fear was valid. If I wanted them to eat, I had to buy food. If I wanted to buy food I needed money. To get money I needed paid work. To get paid work I had to sell jobs.

My buddy eventually reminded me that in between calling him up to worry aloud about not having enough work, I would also call him up regularly to beg him to help me with all the work I suddenly had lined up and now I wasn’t sure how I would get it all done!

Until he put it to me that way, I was too caught up in the moment to realize what I had been doing.

So, which is it? Are we afraid of not having enough? Or are we afraid of having too much?

Let’s not be afraid of either one. Either way, we are adaptable, capable and full of badassity!

Mr. Money Mustache September 26, 2026, 9:09 am

Yeah, it’s not perfect, but I have actually noticed the opposite effect – really good answers to my technical carpentry questions about electrical, plumbing, and even structural work. Apparently it partly depends on what model you’re using.

If you just type a question into Google or whatever, the AI-boosted search mode will give you slightly better answers than the old google would have given. But if you’re using Claude/Gemini/ChatGPT pro and phrase your question specifically, like “is this ground wire connection in my subpanel consistent with the 2024 IRC building code”, and send it a picture of your work, it will do some real building inspector wizardry for you (which I have gone into the code to verify and been shocked at the accuracy). It has already helped me countless times in fixing houses, bikes, cars, whatever.

One other place where it blew my mind – in a recent renovation, I wanted to know if a support beam I found in a ceiling was up to code given the span and the amount of floor it was holding up. It broke down the entire calculation based on floor area, l/360 deflection and all of that and confirmed a bunch of stuff that would have taken me MUCH longer to figure out (or required an expensive email exchange with a structural engineer). I did go in and spot check its numbers, but it got them right. The real value was in finding exactly the right formula for the situation, almost instantly.

If new technology improves productivity, why does everything keep getting more expensive

Mr. Money Mustache September 26, 2026, 2:24 pm

The currency tends to decrease in value, which makes sticker prices higher on a nominal basis. But our incomes have tended to go up more quickly than inflation, which means we can buy more stuff for each hour of our work. To put a number on it, our purchasing power is up about 330% since 1950 – the degree by which wages have outpaced inflation.

The exception is if you’re buying the direct labor of other people in the same rich country, since their hourly rate has gone up too.

More fun details on this, one of my favorite subjects, here! https://www.mrmoneymustache.com/2022/01/11/inflation-should-we-be-worried/

Mr. Money Mustache September 26, 2026, 2:18 pm

You have some valid points there Tim, but don’t confuse my own low-effort AI images with the actual capability of the technology.

For example, not only can it transcribe a meeting but it can then analyze the motivations and emotions of each participant at each point in the meeting, or identify whether there were any contradictions to what the CEO said to you versus what was in the 50-page employment contract you also fed to that AI. These are real cases I’ve been involved in and it was shockingly accurate since I knew the backstory in each case.

Frontier-level AI can also design and execute scientific experiments (or find the tiniest flaws in existing papers), create entire video games that are actually fun to play, teach you almost anything on an individualized basis.. it’s already very close what a high-performing human can do in most fields and beyond humans in some others.

There are lots of problems and risks with the tech, but incompetence at basic tasks is not one of them.

I agree with your overall assessment of AI’s capabilities, but I think the point about pseudo-productivity is legitimate. I’ve been seeing a ton of this at work now. Lots of things that would have been terse, two-sentence emails a couple of years ago are now extremely polished ten-page memorandums that look like they were produced by a McKinsey team. They look impressive when upper management glances at them, but they make no actual contributions to the company’s fundamental productivity and no one actually reads them.

Thanks Pete for the article. I think it would be interesting if you could share more examples of how you use AI and specifically which models/versions. Thanks!

One time I pressed a bit further with questions and we got into things like a descent into autocratic rule, which would lead to a Venezuela-type wipeout of our economy and loss of all our property, and of course there’s the common refrain of “I think I’ll just move to Canada” that many of my fellow Americans tend to say every time the elections go a certain way.

Call me a blind optimist, but I think the USA is going to remain a wealthy and productive economy which respects personal property rights, regardless of which party is in office. Not everything will be perfect, but life will be a lot better the less you read the daily news.

Yes absolutely! I stopped following the news closely several years ago and am way better off, same for social media!

#8443. Pay 0% Tax on Long-Term Gains – Sell and Rebuy in a Low-Income Year

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

In a low-income year, like early retirement or a gap between jobs, you can sell investments held over a year free of federal tax while your taxable income, gains included, stays in the 0% bracket. Sell up to the bracket’s top and buy back what you want to keep, so a later sale is taxed only on newer gains.

This year the IRS puts the top of the 0% bracket for long-term gains, on investments held more than a year, at $98,900 of taxable income for a married couple filing jointly and $49,450 for a single filer. They rise to $102,100 and $51,050 in 2027.

Taxable income is income after deductions. Your room is the top minus the rest of the year’s taxable income, dividends included. Only the profit counts, not the whole sale price, and profit past the top is taxed at the next rate, 15%.

A married couple who retire at 60 with $40,000 of taxable income can sell long-held funds at a $50,000 gain and owe no federal tax on it. On a Marketplace health plan, though, the gain counts as income for the premium tax credit that lowers their premiums. Since 2026, the IRS allows no credit when household income tops 400% of the poverty line, and any advance payments must be repaid.

The 0% rate is federal, so your state may still tax the gain. If you collect Social Security, the gain can make more of your benefits taxable, and each newly taxable dollar shrinks your room by a dollar.

The wash sale rule only blocks deducting a loss when you rebuy within 30 days. So you can buy the shares back the same day and still take the gain at 0%, unless your mutual fund limits quick buybacks. The rebuy resets your cost basis, the figure future gains are measured from, to today’s price. The new shares need more than a year to qualify for the long-term rate again.

Parents can pass this break to a grown child just starting out by giving appreciated shares, which keep your cost basis and the time you’ve held them.

First settle what you’ll take from traditional IRAs or move into a Roth, since both are taxable income that fills the room before gains do. Then sell in December, once the year’s income is clear. The trade date decides which year’s return the gain goes on, so place the sale by the year’s last trading day.

The 101 money moves that matter most, from the foundations to protecting what you’ve built.

#8442. Huge Crowds Trek to Everest Base Camp

The image of an endless line of trekkers heading to Everest Base Camp this week seemed like deja vu. Why are conga lines similar to the infamous ones filmed every May on Everest’s Hillary Step, now repeating at the base of the mountain? We asked the local trekking guide who shot the video.

Ngima Sherpa of Osprey Adventure explained that this is not the norm, but the delay caused by Cyclone Anwar, which hit Nepal last weekend, has created a huge backlog.

“Even though there are always a lot of trekkers heading to Everest Base Camp, this kind of traffic is not usual,” Ngima Sherpa explained. “This video was shot on November 1, after three days of continuous snow and rain…During that time, all the trekkers were stuck in Dingboche, which is two trekking stages away from Everest Base Camp.”

When the weather finally cleared, all those trekkers set off for EBC at the same time, causing heavy traffic on the route from Dingboche to Gorakshep.

The recent cyclone is also the reason why the surroundings are so heavily covered in snow. Usually, the entire trail to Everest Base Camp is dry.

The fresh snow on Nepal’s peaks is having consequences for those planning to summit trekking peaks. Conditions on otherwise straightforward snow slopes have become problematic, as climbers sink into deep, heavy snow.

Satori Adventures had to retreat after swimming in snow on the flanks of Mera Peak, in the Khumbu. “Four to five feet of fresh snow made it impossible to move forward above high camp,” leader Furba Sherpa reported.

Ramesh Gurung noted today that he has retreated from an attempt on 6,119m Lobuche East, another popular Khumbu 6000’er. However, Gurung said he will try again with another group soon.

Conditions will change only as the snow transforms under the dimming sun and freezes in the approaching winter temperatures.

On Ama Dablam, Sherpa climbers struggled to reopen the route to Camp 1 on Tuesday but managed to complete it. The route above Camp 1 follows a steep, mixed ridge with less accumulated snow. Several teams are now on their summit rotations and expect to top out in the next few days.

Tenzing Sherpa of Gokyo Namaste Lodge has shared some useful information for trekkers who plan to cross the huge Ngozumpa Glacier between Gokyo and Dragnag. The recent snowfall makes the usual route less safe, and he recommends following local people along an easier route between Longbanga Tso (1st Lake) and Taujung Tso (2nd Lake). See Tenzing’s sketch of the route:

Alternative route between Gokyo Lakes, suggested by Gokyo Tenzing Sherpa.

Tenzing also noted that the route from Thame to Gokyo is open, as the Gokyo Lodge Association cleared and reopened the trail from the Gokyo side yesterday. However, conditions on the glacier may change quickly.

The route across Gokyo is one of the most popular side trips in the Khumbu, for those wanting to enrich their Everest Base Camp trek. The area features stunning scenery, with high mountain lakes, glaciers, and a view of the formidable South Face of Cho Oyu.

#8441. Store Card Discount at Checkout – Pay in Full and You Keep All of It

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Pay a store card’s balance in full by the first due date, and its checkout discount is yours to keep. If you’d carry the purchase instead, a year of interest at the card’s rate, often above 30%, can cost as much as the discount saves. That’s still cheaper than carrying it on a typical credit card.

Say the clerk offers 15% off a $600 TV if you open the store’s card today. That’s $90 off. But pay off the remaining $510 in 12 equal monthly payments at a 30% annual percentage rate (APR), and by our math the interest comes to about $87.

That leaves you about $3 ahead of paying $600 up front. If you’d carry the TV either way, the store card is still cheaper than a general-purpose card, at the cost of a hard inquiry on your credit report. Those averaged a 25% APR in 2024, the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB) found. By our math, 12 payments on $600 at that rate cost about $84 in interest, so the store card comes out about $88 ahead. Store-only cards averaged 31%, and many charge every cardholder the same rate, so strong credit won’t get you a lower one.

FICO says a hard inquiry takes less than five points off most people’s scores and stops counting after a year. If your credit is strong, a store-only card’s starting limit is typically less than half what a general-purpose card would give you, the CFPB found. So the same $510 balance takes up a bigger share of the limit. That raises your credit utilization, which can lower your score until you pay it down.

After the first purchase, the card is worth using only if you shop there constantly and pay it in full every month. If you shop there only now and then, a no-annual-fee card that earns 2% back on everything you buy is the better everyday card. For the store’s rewards, ask whether it also offers a co-branded card, which many big retailers do. It carries the store’s name but works at other stores too.

Financing that’s interest-free only if you pay in full by a deadline is deferred interest, a different deal with risks of its own.

Take the discount only on something you came to buy, and pay the card off before you charge anything else to it.

The 101 money moves that matter most, from the foundations to protecting what you’ve built.

#8440. Animal Attack Roundup, Nov. 4

While they predominate the media reports, most animal attack victims are not tourists unwisely attempting to take selfies with wildlife. People who live and work every day among animals are the ones who most often face the consequences.

As expanding populations push human settlements further into wildlife habitat, we can expect to see more conflicts. Here are some recent incidents.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Tisha Mukherjee, CC BY-SA 4.0

In Indonesia, 24-year-old Selviana Ambu Kaka was attacked by a crocodile while washing clothes in the Bondo Kodi River. Her fiancé and his sister witnessed a large crocodile emerging from the water, biting Ambu Kaka on the leg, and dragging her into the river. They immediately alerted nearby relatives and authorities, and a search commenced.

The people of Southwest Sumba, the closest town, organized and combed the area with nets. When they found a large crocodile matching witness descriptions, they hauled it onto shore and killed it. The animal measured nearly 4.9 meters long.

Early on the morning of October 24, Ambu Kaka’s remains were found approximately 30m from the attack site. She is one of many such victims in Indonesia, which sees more saltwater crocodile attacks than any other country.

Habitat destruction is largely behind the massive number of human-crocodile conflicts. It’s an understandably hard sell, however, for conservation workers to convince locals that preventing attacks means protecting the perpetrators.

The victim had recently returned from Jakarta to become formally engaged on October 30.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/ Rohit Gangwal, CC BY-SA 4.0

A 65-year-old farm laborer, Tulsi Rama Sawar, was killed by a leopard in state of Maharashtra, just north of Mumbai, on November 1. The noise of the attack summoned help from the locals. By the time they arrived, the leopard had fled, but Sawar was deceased. Her case comes less than a month after a leopard killed a 52-year-old woman in the same area.

Town residents are demanding immediate government action to capture the animal. If their demands for cage traps and cameras are not granted, locals threaten collective protest. The attacks represent a threat to their lives, but also their livelihoods, as farmers are unable to work the fields out of fear of attack.

Elsewhere, in Uttar Pradesh near the Nepal border, a 45-year-old farmer named Kennu fought off an attacking leopard, sustaining serious injuries. The man was working in a sugarcane field when the predator leaped out from hiding. Kennu fought back, and the animal retreated long enough for him to flee home, where family members brought him to medical care. Forestry officials have urged agricultural workers to avoid working in the fields alone.

Farm workers are not the only leopard victims, however. Early in the morning of November 4, a 15-year-old girl was dragged from her home in the Shahdol district of central India.

Saraswati Singh Gond was sleeping in bed beside her sister on their veranda, which did not have a door. A leopard entered during the night, not even waking her sister. Singh Gond’s body was found approximately 300m from the house. The search for the leopard continues.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Shadow Ayush, CC BY-SA 4.0

Leopards are only one of the wildlife threats that rural Indians face. A 55-year-old woman was gathering mushrooms in Utterakand in northern India on October 30 when she was attacked and killed by a wild elephant. Devaki Rana was lifted up and thrown to the ground, her son reported.

The incident occurred in one of India’s elephant corridors, which allow highly endangered Asian elephant herds to move between their fragmented habitats. The corridors are essential to conservation, but also present a concentrated area where attacks are more common.

Another fatal encounter took place in the state of Chhattisgarh in east-central India. A man of between 35 and 40 years of age, traveling by motorcycle with his nephew, came upon an elephant standing in the road. It immediately charged them, killing the uncle, while his nephew managed to hide beneath a parked truck. Eventually, the elephant left, and he was able to escape.

Locals believe that the elephant in question is one known as Kabra Kaan, which they see frequently and which has been connected with earlier incidents. The Forestry Department, however, has not confirmed this.

Elephant corridors allow them to pass between the remaining forested areas. Photo: Wildlife Trust of India

In Japan, brown bear attacks have been rising for several years. As in many places, human settlements are encroaching on brown bear territory. A decrease in farmland has left no boundary between the urban and the forested worlds. The government has responded with controversial bear culls, despite the concerns of conservation workers.

The latest incident is very different from the increasingly common attack stories. Late on the night of October 30, an express train running on Hokkaido’s Soya Line collided with a large brown bear. The body of the bear was trapped under the train, and the wheels derailed.

A brown bear became trapped beneath the wheels of Sarobetsu No 3. Photo: Hokkaido Railway Company

None of the crew or 32 passengers were injured in the crash. However, fearing more bears were in the area, officials kept everyone on the train overnight, until they could be safely extracted the following morning. Company officials have confirmed that this is the first bear-collision derailment in their nearly 40-year history.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8439. Young Climbers Bag New Routes, Including First Ascent, in Rolwaling

An energetic foursome of young French climbers had a busy time recently in Nepal’s Rolwaling Valley, east of the Khumbu. Eric Bazon, Pierre Girot, Sophie Jacob, and Kilian Moni did two new routes, including a first ascent of a 5,205m rocky spire and one very difficult line. They then climbed another peak on a “rest” day.

As soon as the climbers reached Rolwaling, they had to change their primary goal, which was in poor condition. Their acclimatization was also progressing more slowly than expected. Instead, they focused their attention on a ridge they saw from their base camp every morning. On October 20, Bazon, Jacob, and Girot set off toward the summit, while Moni waited for them at the base.

“Before long, the climbing turned vertical, and the ridge became razor-sharp and exposed,” Bazon wrote. The climb took three days on the ridge, bivvies included, and led Bazon, Sophie Jacob, and Pierre Girot to the west summit of ChekiGo (6,128m). It took another full day and one more bivouac to reach base camp.

Cheki Go’s main summit was first climbed in 2005 by Bruce Normand of the UK and Oliver Von Rotz of Switzerland via the west face. A Spanish team led by Mikel Zabalza first reached the west summit via a direct route up the west face in 2017. The team had just returned from bagging the first ascent of Jugal II.

“It was quite an intense experience for a ‘secondary’ project: one that combined rock climbing on mediocre granite, improvised protection, and steep snow ridges, all while hauling our ropes on our backs,” said Bazon.

The ridge was technical enough to be a good start: 5c on rock and M4 on mixed terrain. In Alpine grades, the team assessed it as TD (very difficult).

Since the weather forecast looked good for another four days, the team decided to continue climbing. They split into two teams: Bazon and Girot headed for a peak previously climbed by elite American alpinists Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey in 2014: Chukyima Go (6,259m). Like their predecessors, the French team approached the west face from the Naa Valley in Rolwaling.

“There was this direct, narrow, and obvious couloir that simply begged to be climbed,” Bazon reported.

The climbers at an open bivy. Photo: Eric Bazon

The next morning, they found the west face in great condition, which allowed them to climb a direct route to the summit along rock cracks, steep ice, and varied mixed terrain.

It took them two days (October 27-28), with one cold bivy on the face with their feet in their backpacks, to complete the route’s 23 pitches. Their West Face Direct route is 900m long, and the team assessed it as ED (extremely difficult), with M6 on mixed sections, and an Alpine ice grade of AI5.

The photo below shows that the new French route, TchikitaMa Go, runs to the right of the previous line opened by the two Americans.

Left: the new route opened by the French team, compared, right, to the 2014 line by Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey. Photo: Eric Bazon/PlanetMountain

Meanwhile, Sophie Jacob and Kilian Moni went for an unclimbed peak that also rose right in front of their base camp: 5,205m Janjmu. The pair completed the first ascent on an aesthetic 600m line up the north face. They called the route The Eternal Stars (“Les etoiles eternelles”) after some friends who had recently perished.

“It felt just like being at home in the Alps, with the added challenge of high altitude,” the climbers reported. “We pushed ourselves to the limit, both physically and mentally, across demanding yet incredibly logical pitches.”

But this was still not enough for the pair. They squeezed in a final climb of Yalung Ri in what they called “a contemplative, active recovery” day. They then finished the trip by paragliding from the summit.

Kilian Moni flies from the top of Yalung Ri. Photo: Kilian Moni

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8438. 20 Ways to Save Money as a Family – Child Care, Food & Kids’ Costs

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

For parents who pay for child care, it’s usually the biggest cost that comes with kids, so that’s where your family should start saving. Check for free public pre-K and state child care assistance before you pay full price, use tax breaks on what’s left, then cut food and kids’ costs.

Child Care Aware of America puts the national average price of child care at $13,184 a year in 2025, about 10% of a two-parent household’s median income and 33% of a single parent’s. Feeding more people adds up as well, and so do the clothes, gear, activities, and parties kids outgrow or ask for again every year.

Each move below cuts one of those costs and works on its own, so you can take them one bill at a time. For rent, insurance, and other bills every household pays, the ways to save money on a tight budget still apply.

Unlike a tax break, which only trims part of the bill, a free seat can take a paid year of preschool off your costs entirely.

Before you pay for preschool, check whether your state or city offers free public pre-K, and apply as soon as applications open, often months ahead. If you still need a paid program, compare co-op and part-day preschools, then pay with pretax money from a dependent care flexible spending account or claim the child and dependent care credit.

Read more: Free Public Pre-K Can Replace a Year of Preschool Tuition - Check Before You Pay

Assistance helps pay the daycare bill itself, which suits a family short on cash better than a tax credit that pays back only once you file.

If you work or attend school or job training, apply to your state’s child care assistance program even if you think you earn too much, and do it early, since many states keep waiting lists. For a child under 5, apply to free Head Start or Early Head Start as well.

Read more: You May Qualify for Child Care Assistance - Some States Help Middle-Class Families

Up to a yearly limit, what you still pay for care after the free and subsidized options can come out of a dependent care account at work.

If you pay for daycare, preschool, or day camp so you can work, enroll in your employer’s dependent care flexible spending account (FSA) at open enrollment. Money you put in skips federal income and payroll taxes, so in the 22% bracket each dollar of care costs you about 70 cents. Elect only what you’re certain to spend.

Read more: Dependent Care FSA - Pay for Daycare Before Income & Payroll Taxes

After-school care and summer day camp can count toward the credit for kids under 13, so it keeps paying long after the pre-K years end.

If you pay for daycare, preschool, a nanny, or day camp so you can work, claim the child and dependent care credit when you file. It’s worth 20% to 50% of up to $3,000 in care costs for one child under 13, or $6,000 for two or more, minus any tax-free dependent care benefits from work.

Read more: Child and Dependent Care Credit - Middle-Income Families Now Get 35%, Up From 20%

Parents who want care at home rather than at a center get the most from it, and it runs most smoothly with a family a few minutes away.

Hire one nanny together with another family, and each family usually pays about two-thirds of a nanny’s going rate while the nanny earns a third more. Put the nanny share’s terms in writing first, including how much notice a family gives before leaving, and pay the payroll taxes on your own share.

Read more: Share a Nanny With Another Family - Each Pays Less, the Nanny Earns More

Evenings out and weekend appointments need care too, and trading hours pays for that care with your time instead of a sitter’s fee.

A babysitting co-op lets you trade hours of watching other families’ kids for hours of free sitting when you need it, for date nights or appointments. Join one near you or start one with five to 15 families you trust, track the hours in points, and cap how many points any family can owe.

Read more: Babysitting Co-op - Trade Hours With Other Families Instead of Paying a Sitter

Planning dinners ahead decides what your family buys before you reach the store, which puts every grocery trip on a budget from the start.

Before you shop, spend 20 minutes planning the week’s dinners from what’s on sale and what’s already in your kitchen. Pick five dinners that share ingredients, save a night for leftovers, keep a backup meal on hand, and write your grocery list from that plan. You’ll make fewer trips and order less takeout.

Read more: Family Meal Planning on a Budget - Let the Week's Sales Set the Menu

In weeks when grocery money runs short before payday, this move matters most, and it works alongside free school meals and other food help rather than in place of them.

When food money is tight, build your family’s meals around a dozen cheap staples, such as rice, dried beans, eggs, and potatoes, and cook them from scratch instead of buying boxed or frozen dinners. Make enough at dinner to pack the next day’s lunches.

Read more: Feed a Family on a Tight Budget - Cook From a Dozen Cheap Staples

As a family grows, the biggest package looks like the obvious buy, and a glance at the shelf tag on each grocery run shows when it isn’t.

Before you grab the family size or the sale item, check the unit price on the shelf tag, which gives the cost per ounce, count, or sheet. It’s the only fair way to compare packages of different sizes, and when both tags use the same unit, it shows in seconds whether the bigger or discounted package costs less.

Read more: Unit Pricing - The Family Size Isn't Always the Cheaper Buy

Once kids reach school age, a free or reduced-price school lunch cuts the cost of one meal every school day, right through high school.

Before you pay for or pack another school lunch, find out whether your kids can get free or reduced-price meals. Your school or state may already feed every student free, and kids in families on SNAP food benefits get free meals automatically. Otherwise, fill out the school’s meal application each school year, or whenever your income drops.

Read more: School Lunch Can Cost 40 Cents or Nothing - Check Whether Your Kids Qualify

Formula and baby food are costs only a young family carries, and WIC helps pay for them in the years before school starts.

If you’re pregnant, recently pregnant, or raising a child under 5, WIC provides healthy food, infant formula, and breastfeeding help. In most states the income limit for a household of three is $50,542 a year before taxes, and Medicaid or SNAP enrollment qualifies you on income automatically. Apply through your local WIC office.

Read more: Pregnant or Raising a Baby? Medicaid or SNAP Already Meets WIC's Income Rule

Dinners out don’t have to disappear from a family budget, and this move keeps them in it while your kids are young enough to qualify.

Several family chains let kids eat free one or two days a week, usually with one free kids’ meal for each paying adult entrée, and a couple let young kids eat free any day. Plan your family dinners out around those days, and confirm your location runs the deal before you go.

Read more: Kids Eat Free at 10 Chain Restaurants - Each Deal's Day, Age Cutoff & Catch

Every growth spurt brings the clothing bill back, and secondhand prices keep that repeat cost small without asking your kids to go without.

Buy most of your kids’ clothes secondhand, since children outgrow clothes long before wearing them out. Start with a kids’ consignment sale, a pop-up event usually held each spring and fall where local families sell what their children outgrew at a fraction of the new price. Then fill gaps at resale shops and by swapping with other families.

Read more: Buy Kids' Clothes Secondhand - Shop the Spring & Fall Consignment Sales First

Clearance covers the pieces you’d rather buy new, such as a winter coat, and works alongside secondhand shopping instead of replacing it.

New kids’ clothes cost the least at end-of-season clearance, but your child’s current size likely won’t fit when that season comes back. Shop for next year instead, usually one size up, and bring a list of what each child still needs so the markdowns fill real gaps.

Read more: Kids' Clothes on Clearance - Buy Next Year's Size, Not This Year's

Gear is the first big bill a new baby brings, and you face it before the baby even arrives.

Most baby gear lasts longer than your baby needs it, so borrow it or buy it used after checking for recalls and missing parts. Buy the car seat and crib mattress new, and take a used crib only if it was made after June 28, 2011. Use your registry’s completion discount on what you buy new.

Read more: Borrow or Buy Used Baby Gear - but Get the Car Seat & Mattress New

Borrowing toys saves money and the shelf space they’d fill, and it lets your kids try new ones as often as their interests change.

Toys for babies and toddlers fit a stage your child soon outgrows, so borrow them instead of buying. Start with your public library or a toy library, then try swap groups and rental services, and rotate the few favorites you keep so they feel new again.

Read more: Borrow the Toys Your Kids Will Outgrow - Libraries Lend Them for Little or Nothing

Each child in school brings a new supply list every year, so the savings multiply in a family with more than one student.

Before you buy school supplies, gather last year’s leftovers, from backpacks and binders to calculators and pens, and cross everything that still works off the teacher’s list. Then buy only what’s still missing, and get the basics during July’s back-to-school sales, when folders, notebooks, and crayons can cost under a dollar each.

Read more: Shop Your House Before Buying School Supplies - What You Reuse Beats Any Sale

Activity costs can climb every season, and this move keeps the yearly total down while your kids keep playing.

For the average family, most of what a child’s sport costs goes to travel, lessons, camps, and gear, not registration. Start your child in a rec league or community program, buy sports gear used, rent musical instruments, ask about financial aid, and keep paid activities to one or two a season.

Read more: Kids' Activities Cost Less in Rec Leagues - Skip the Travel, Buy Gear Used

Birthdays come once a year for each child, so a family with several kids throws several parties, and the savings from keeping each one simple add up.

Host your child’s birthday party at home or in a park instead of booking a trampoline park or play place. Keep the guest list to your child’s age plus one, serve pizza and a homemade or grocery-store cake, and trade rented entertainment and goody bags for a game the kids play together.

Read more: Throw a Kids' Birthday Party at Home - Invite Fewer Guests and Skip Goody Bags

Summer break gives kids the most free time of the year, and planning those weeks ahead keeps most days from costing anything.

Keep your kids busy until school starts without a big bill by repeating a few cheap outings every week, such as a discounted summer movie, a free library program, and a trip to the splash pad or pool. Sign up early for free programs that fill up, and let the kids help spend a small fun budget.

Read more: 20 Cheap Summer Activities for Kids - Most Are Free With a Little Planning

Together, these moves trim the costs that come with kids, a little on each bill and the most on child care. Start this week by looking up your state’s free pre-K program and child care assistance, then apply to whichever your family qualifies for.

The 101 money moves that matter most, from the foundations to protecting what you’ve built.

So every provider here charges no maintenance fee and has no minimum to start investing.

Payroll deposits into a work HSA generally skip Social Security and Medicare taxes, so keep that account for them and move money from it to an HSA you choose. #8436. Duo Completes Baffin Island Circuit By Skiing, Hiking, and Packrafting

Caleb McDaniels, 25, and Henry Penfold, 22, have completed their impressive expedition around Baffin Island. The Americans combined skiing, hiking, and packrafting to almost circle the world’s fifth-largest island. McDaniels told ExplorersWeb they did about 20% skiing and the rest was evenly split between backpacking and packrafting.

The pair began and ended in Iqaluit, Nunavut’s capital. The final section of their trip was also the longest, a 1,130km stretch with no villages after Igloolik to resupply. They made light work of it, knocking it out in 40 days and bringing their total distance to 3,262km over 145 days, from their late May start to their return to Iqaluit in mid-October. By then, the oncoming Arctic winter gave them few hours of light to work with.

The pair’s completed route around Baffin Island. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

With no resupply points, the pair left Igloolik with 82kg of food. They hoped to do it in seven weeks but brought even more food, because they weren’t sure how long that giant giant section would actually take, explained Caleb McDaniels.

“With no way to carry that much weight in our packs, we loaded up our packrafts and paddled the first 400 miles [644km]. Then, for the final 300 miles [483km], we heaved on our packs to walk overland,” they explained.

The paddling portion went quickly, aided by some ingenuity. “Much of the time, we were able to sail using our trekking poles as masts and panels cut out of an old tent for sails. Speeding along at over five mph, we did some incredible downwinding, spray flying from our bows.”

Using makeshift sails. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They also effectively halved the 480km trek by packrafting over two large lakes, including Nettilling, the world’s largest lake on an island. Yet there was no way to ease the final 160km slog into Iqaluit.

Postholing through early winter snow back to Iqaluit. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

“It was more of a postholing session — we slowly picked our way across snow-covered boulder fields and partly frozen marshlands. Constantly breaking through thin ice over the tundra marshes, we walked in our drysuits to keep our legs and feet dry. Each morning required half an hour of chipping ice and melting out our shoes, which invariably froze solid,” they said.

McDaniels and Penfold had planned a roughly circular route around the island, but “we weren’t trying to circumnavigate Baffin,” McDaniels told ExplorersWeb. Two previous parties had circumnavigated the island by dogteam, but McDaniels and Penfold’s route was driven by aesthetic considerations rather than to satisfy a catchy trip definition.

Slogging overland. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They chose their route after looking at satellite images and enjoyed both spectacular scenery and some special wildlife sightings, including at least one that was a little too close for comfort.

“One morning, as I went to fill our water bottles, I stumbled upon a sleeping polar bear hidden behind a low rise. Only a few yards away, it lifted its massive white head and glared at me for a moment before returning to its nap in the sun,” McDaniels wrote. “I carefully retreated behind the rise and yelled over to Henry to get the shotgun and flares out just in case. We quickly packed our boats and got on the water.”

In Foxe Basin, they almost collided with a sleeping walrus, and on Nettilling Lake, they met one of the resident seals. “He was immensely curious and not at all afraid. He swam around our boats for half an hour, playing with our stern lines and swimming back and forth between us. At one point, I put my hand into the water, and he brushed it with his nose!”

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

#8435. Americans Bag Major First Ascent in Pakistan

Americans Dane Steadman and Cody Winckler, awarded with a Piolet d’Or in 2025 after the first ascent of Yashkuk Sar, have done a similar monster 1,800m-long new route on the north face of Sosbun Brakk (6,414m). This time, Grant Stewart joined them for this off-season climb in the Karakoram.

This first ascent of the peak’s north face included difficulties up to M7, WI5. The climbers had to overcome several obstacles before even setting foot on the face. Like many other alpine-style teams this season, they were originally planning to climb another peak. But instead of adapting because of washed-out trails, they had bureaucratic issues. Their permit, obtained 10 months earlier, was revoked some weeks before their departure. They had to quickly find a different goal.

Meanwhile, the airline misplaced Winckler’s baggage — every adventurer’s nightmare. Somehow, the outfitter sent it to base camp some days later. Then, Steadman had to overcome serious Acute Mountain Sickness in base camp. Their acclimatization was hampered by freak spells of bad weather. Finally, the line on Sosbun Brakk they initially chose was too hard and risky because of a hanging serac they only spotted after they began.

The team’s lonely base camp. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Back at BC, it was time to rest, regroup, and contemplate a totally different line that we’d seen from on the wall, which gave us new hope,” Steadman recalled.

Off to the north face. Photo: Dane Steadman

On October 1, the three climbers set off on that potential new line. “Instead of angling left up hard mixed ground above the cone as before, we made an arcing traverse right, across an ice apron, to gain runnels on the right side of the face,” Steadman explained.

The team gained 800m on that first day and set their first bivouac on a snowy arete, just in time to be slammed by a violent storm that kept them in their tent the whole next day.

Steep mixed terrain. Photo: Dane Steadman

A tarp to protect the team’s bivouac from falling spindrift. Photo: Dane Steadman

“In the morning, we made a gamble for the top by leaving the tent and starting up a massive gully system,” Steward wrote. “At its top, we reached another dead end in a huge serac barrier. To escape this, Dane committed to a visionary pitch, casting off up a blank slab to eventually reach an ice smear.”

Chaotic, difficult rocky sections. Photo: Dane Steadman

“A huge serac to our right and smooth slabs out left posed a serious question; eight meters of sideways teetering on tiny gneissic edges and a miraculous rock thread were the answer,” said Steadman.

“We continued up the massive hanging glacier to reach the hallucinatory final headwall of steep ice/mixed climbing and mushroom-weaving lunacy,” Stewart continued. “One final traverse with 1,800m of exposure under our feet and a magically appearing, dead-vertical ice tongue led us to the summit ridge.”

A totally vertical section. Photo: Grant Stewart

“The wild terrain on the upper mountain took longer than we wanted,” Steadman admitted. By the time they finally reached the summit, it was 4 pm, the storm was coming the following day, and they still had a long way (and many rappels) down.

Summit picture on Sosbun Brakk. Left to right, Dane Steadman, Grant Stewart, and Cody Wrincker. Photo: Dane Steadman

“The sun would set around 6 pm, so we threaded the first of 35 rappels and started down. Long after dark, we finally arrived back at our bivy ledge at around 5,800m,” Steadman recalled.

The storm was not supposed to start until noon the following day, but it was already snowing at 8 am, with still 1,200 vertical meters of descent to go.

Rappelling down the route. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Before long, we were caught in a proper storm,” Stewart explained. “We committed to a faster and hopefully safer descent by rappelling down the west rib to the Sokha/Sosbun col. By the time we reached the col, we could barely communicate over the screaming southwest wind.”

After some scary rappels in high winds down the serac band, the climbers finally reached a sheltered section of the face. They still had to deal with heavy spindrift coming from above until finally, at around 4 pm, they touched down on the glacier. And yet, the adventure was not over — especially for Grant Stewart:

Following Dane, I took maybe 10 steps when I felt the ground under me crack. Instinctively, I jumped and landed on my stomach as a huge crevasse swallowed the ground behind me. Soaking wet, we began postholing into the wan light of a mesmerizing white out.

[We stuck] to the center of the glacier as avalanches rumbled down all around in the whirling snow. After a strange eternity, the ragged colors of a tent appeared and we were greeted by ecstatic dancing, big hugs, and endless cups of chai from (our BC crew) Ibrahim and Idrees.

Happy climbers and base camp crew after a safe return from the summit. Photo: Dane Steadman

The team didn’t report their success and the details of the climb until they returned home. And then, the first words of appraisal were not for the route itself, but for the mountains and people of Gilgit-Baltistan.

“It’s a unique place…where continents and cultures collide. Trips there, while often difficult, are rewarding in ways hard to describe simply,” Steadman said. This was his third trip to the Karakoram, a place he has fallen in love with.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

Every dollar you permanently eliminate from your spending reduces the amount you need to reach FI. But assuming your income stays the same, that money is also available to invest. You're building your portfolio faster while simultaneously lowering the finish line you're trying to reach.

#8433. Weekend Warm-Up: Assynt

Assynt, subtitled “Home is where the croft is,” is a short documentary that brings viewers to the Scottish Highland community of Assynt, where residents live on traditional crofts.

The croft is a uniquely Scottish term, describing the smaller, less fertile plots of land that poorer people were forced onto, following the infamous Highland Clearances of the 18th and 19th centuries. Crofters lived on their crofts and farmed for subsistence, grazing sheep on shared land. Unable to support themselves on the croft alone, most also worked in fishing, kelp, or peat to support themselves.

It was a very hard life. But Lewis MacAskill, whom the viewer is soon introduced to, doesn’t see it that way. He is one of just over a thousand residents of Assynt, who chose to stay and continue the crofting tradition.

“I regard the croft as an extension of who I am as a person,” MacAskill explains.

The small community of Assynt. Photo: Screenshot

MacAskill introduces us to some of the other members of the community. Anna is a sheepdog owner and trainer, whom we see herding sheep using the same techniques people here have used for centuries. Stuart is a passionate fisherman, who shares his love of Assynt’s natural landscape with his young son.

We also meet some of the creatives who have, MacAskill says, been drawn by the unique landscape and material culture. We see a potter using local materials, a fiddler, and a spinner on her spinning wheel.

The spinning wheel came to Europe from the Middle East by the 13th century, and was widely adopted by the 18th century, replacing the older drop spindle and distaff. Photo: Screenshot

MacAskill moved to the croft a decade ago, and doesn’t regret it. Having grown up visiting every summer, he has always felt drawn to the land and knows others feel the same. But a small, isolated community like Assynt is constantly in danger of depopulation. MacAskill hopes that people keep choosing to move back and stay, heeding the same call he did.

The film ends on this note, with a montage of scenes from life in Assynt and the landscape it sits in, while an elderly crofter sings a traditional Scottish song of homecoming.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.
