#8432. Getting Rich Is Easy Compared To Finding And Keeping Love

As a parent, I have two main concerns for my children.

The first is economic stability. Will they be able to earn a livable wage in a world where AI keeps eating entry-level jobs? I question the time and money we spend educating our kids all the time. So I've been aggressively saving and investing for their future, just in case.

The second concern is bigger. Will my children find love?

A net worth target gets more achievable the more you save and the more time goes by. Compounding is boring, but it's reliable. If the direction is correct, sooner or later you will get there.

Finding a partner is far more amorphous. Our kids can work on their communication skills, fitness, charm, education, hygiene, empathy, and finances. But all of that merely improves their odds. There are no guarantees.

Which brings me to the strangest business story of 2026.

In late October, The New York Times reported that U.S. officials concluded a Chinese citizen named Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a Chinese intelligence asset. She is a senior executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based data center company and Nvidia cloud partner that rents computing power to Chinese firms.

Surveillance of her reportedly revealed a close relationship with Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. And no, they are not related. Huang is a common surname like Smith.

The Financial Times then reported that a U.S. government memo described the relationship as romantic. Her alleged goal was to gather information on Jensen and advanced AI chips, and to nudge him to lobby for looser chip exports to China.

The allegations come from anonymous officials and haven't been tested publicly. Officials reportedly don't believe Jensen knew about any intelligence activity. Nvidia says his interactions with her were routine customer meetings. China denies the whole thing. House Republican committee chairs want a closed-door briefing on November 18.

So nobody outside the room knows the full story, except that Jensen admitted to the affair and is no longer CEO as of November 23, 2026. The lessons about love and money are worth discussing regardless of how the facts shake out.

Jensen Huang is worth over $120 billion (once $170 billion). He runs a company worth about $4 trillion after the sell-off. He is the closest thing we have to a modern-day emperor, the face of the AI revolution, mobbed like a rock star everywhere he goes in Taiwan.

And yet, if the reporting is accurate, it still wasn't enough.

There's a line from Gemma Teller Morrow in the TV show, Sons of Anarchy: “Only men need to be loved, sweetheart. Women need to be wanted.”

It's not entirely true. Everybody wants to be loved and desired to some degree. But the line points to something uncomfortable. No amount of money, applause, or leather jackets fills the need to feel admired by one specific person who seems to see you.

We all just want to be loved and accepted for who we are.

Here's the twist that makes this story sadder.

If U.S. officials are right, what may have felt like admiration was tradecraft. Attentiveness, respect, and flattery are exactly what an intelligence service would deploy against the most important man in AI. Intelligence services have used romance as an opening for as long as there have been secrets worth stealing.

Which exposes the hidden problem of extreme wealth and power. The richer you get, the harder it becomes to know whether anyone loves you for you. Every new person in your life has a reason to be nice to you. Normal people get to skip that existential headache.

Money doesn't make finding love easier. Past a certain point, it makes find true love harder.

The people worth keeping are the ones who liked you before you were worth liking.

Jensen is far from the first emperor to create a commotion over love for a woman.

Paris and Helen of Troy. The Greeks' foundational story is about a prince who ran off with another man's wife and got his entire city burned to the ground. Myth, sure. But the Greeks clearly understood the risk.

Mark Antony and Cleopatra. Antony controlled half the Roman world. He chose Cleopatra over Rome, lost at Actium in 31 BC, and both were dead within a year. Octavian got the empire.

King Edward VIII. In 1936, he abdicated the British throne after less than a year to marry Wallis Simpson, a twice-divorced American. He traded a kingdom for love, and spent the rest of his life in comfortable, irrelevant exile.

John Profumo. The closest parallel to the Jensen story. In 1963, Britain's Secretary of State for War was revealed to have had an affair with Christine Keeler, who was also involved with a Soviet naval attaché. Profumo lied to Parliament, resigned in disgrace, and the scandal helped bring down the government.

Brian Krzanich. Intel's CEO resigned in 2018 after a past consensual relationship with an employee came to light. Running a chip giant doesn't come with an exemption.

Jeff Bezos. His 2019 divorce after 25 years of marriage sent roughly 4% of Amazon, worth about $38 billion at the time, to MacKenzie Scott. Bezos kept his job, married Lauren Sánchez, and is still one of the richest men alive. But it was the most expensive breakup in history.

The pattern is clear. Kingdoms, careers, and fortunes are rebuildable. However, once you find the one, you will do anything to get them.

Remember Indecent Proposal from 1993? Robert Redford’s billionaire character offers a struggling couple, played by Demi Moore and Woody Harrelson, $1 million for one night with the wife. They take the deal. Their marriage nearly implodes.

The couple discovered they had a price, and it wasn’t that high. But they badly underestimated what they were selling.

Meanwhile, the billionaire could buy the night. He couldn’t buy her heart. He had everything except the one thing he actually wanted.

Swap Redford for any modern tech billionaire and the lesson holds. Money can buy almost anything. Except the feeling of being genuinely loved.

Plenty of regular people have become millionaires this way. The formula is public.

There is no formula for love. You can do everything right and still never meet the one. And even if you do, roughly 40% of first marriages in America end in divorce. Finding love involves tremendous luck. Keeping it is work.

You can always make another dollar. You can't always make another soulmate.

In 2023, the U.S. Surgeon General declared loneliness an epidemic. About half of American adults report feeling lonely, and the health impact of social disconnection was compared to smoking up to 15 cigarettes a day.

The FIRE crowd isn't immune. Early retirement can be isolating. Your friends are at work. Your identity is gone. And if you're married, there's a specific trap worth sharing.

FIRE is better when both partners retire and experience freedom together. Not when one spouse is slaving away while the other is kicking back. Resentment will surely kick in.

Yes, I get it. Some men’s egos still need protecting when their wives are bringing home the sashimi. But men need to get over themselves. Women now make up the majority of college students, and plenty of them will out-earn their husbands.

I left banking in 2012. My wife kept working until 2015 when she negotiated her own severance. For almost three years, I was the guy at home while she commuted.

If you're planning FIRE, plan a joint exit. Life is better this way.

1) A high-value spouse may need more respect just to break even. If your partner is successful, attractive, or powerful, the world is constantly praising them. Next to all that adoration, treating them normally at home can inadvertently feel like a lack of respect, or even disrespect. So you may have to deliberately show more appreciation than feels natural just to run in place.

2) Everything gets taken for granted. Eat Kobe beef every day and you’ll get sick of it. In FIRE land, wake up without an alarm clock every day and you’ll take that for granted too. The antidote is voluntary discomfort. Scrub your own toilets after a big investment win. Take a part-time consulting gig a 40-minute bus ride away that makes you take orders from a micromanager. Practice fasting for at least 18 hours once a month if you have access to unlimited food. Doing uncomfortable things on purpose helps you appreciate the people you already have.

Whatever the full truth turns out to be, this whole situation is sad. Sad for Lori and their adult children. Sad for Jensen and his legacy. Disappointing for Nvidia’s tens of thousands of employees and shareholders like me. And a blow for America, given we’re in a global AI race.

Relationships are hard. They require constant work and attentiveness. Forty years of marriage doesn’t buy you immunity. If anything, it makes it easier to stop paying attention.

If there’s one silver lining, it’s that this story may help bring the left and the right together on maintaining our lead in AI. AI spending has been one of the biggest drivers of U.S. economic growth. If post-midterm infighting causes us to lose the race, we could sink the economy along with it.

If someone with that much money and goodwill was allegedly willing to risk throwing it away, you can’t really blame people for their emotions and their desire to be loved. We’re all human.

Besides a basic amount of financial security, I hope my kids will find someone who will support, love, and cherish them. I would rather my kids find that person than be rich.

Money can help protect them after I’m gone. But money can’t hug them after a bad day, sit with them in a hospital waiting room, or celebrate their wins with them. Knowing they each have someone like that is what will let me rest easy. That’s when I’ll know my kids will be OK.

Then I hope they never take that person for granted. Because the odds say they will, just like most of us take for granted the water we drink and the air we breathe.

Readers, do you agree that making money is easier than finding love? Would you take the $1 million in Indecent Proposal, or $2.2 million in today's dollars? If you've achieved FIRE, did you and your spouse exit together, or did one of you keep working, and how did that affect your relationship? And if you had $140+ billion, how would you ever know if someone loved you for you?

If you're a worried parent, I'm diligently working on my new book out in Fall 2027, Your Kids Will Be OK: Smart Money Decisions For Parents In An Uncertain World, published by Portfolio Penguin. It's about raising capable, resilient kids and making solid money decisions so they have a real shot at independence, and at a good life.

You can’t buy love, but you can protect it. If your family depends on your income, term life insurance is one of the most loving financial moves you can make.

My wife and I each got matching 20-year term policies through Policygenius after shopping around for months. It took far less time than I expected, and we sleep better knowing our kids would be OK if the unthinkable happened. Check out Policygenius to compare quotes from top insurers for free.

Stocks can swing 20% on a single headline. Real estate tends to move more slowly, generate rental income, and give you something tangible to own. That’s why real estate has been a core part of my net worth for over 23 years, and it helps me sleep better when the stock market gets volatile.

Jensen represented the diversity and pure achievement representing one of the racial backgrounds in America that should be elevated and promoted across corporate America, as opposed to the supposed good old boy fraternity culture.

Instead, we have someone who embodied the same abuses of power and the appearance of a proclivity to profit and aid direct competition in China using loopholes to sell chips that ended up there. Also, if the “honeypot” was a spy, how do we know Jensen wasn’t being used or complicit, there needs to be a thorough investigation of this. Could his Chinese and Taiwanese heritage to want to establish more connection with like people, as is accused of other races in power?

Also, quarterly before a 10k filing CEOs have to answer many questions including “Have there been any violations of the company’s Code of Business Conduct and Ethics (such as fraternization policies, conflicts of interest, or improper expense reimbursements)?” This is a CEO having affairs with at least two employees, that is grounds for immediate termination.

He is a married man, who has found and committed to love including having children with his spouse. If he needed erotic love, he should have chosen to commit adultery outside of his work, and not with a female executive whose business is to funnel AI chips to China.

At the least, Jensen has admitted to extramarital affairs and misleading company officials regarding a relationship with an executive suspected by U.S. intelligence of being a Chinese spy. At the worst, he facilitated illegal export-control violations and espionage. Let’s not minimize this into a poor hard working founder just looking for love in all the wrong places.

The distinction between financial stability and a lasting relationship is thought-provoking. Communication and empathy can grow with practice, but relationships also depend on choices both people make over time.

With so many divorces over infidelity, has anyone stopped to consider that the problem might actually lie with the institution of marriage itself? The dopamine rush of love only lasts two or three years.

Forcing yourself to feel butterflies for just one person for fifty years goes against your genes.

In the past, people could grow old together—partly because they didn’t live very long, and partly because they couldn’t survive on their own after a divorce.

Now we can survive without being tied to someone, yet our lifespans have doubled, and we still expect the same person to be our roommate, teammate, confidant, and lover.

The old contract from the agricultural era simply can’t contain a modern person’s much longer life….

In-laws – Boundaries with parents, family involvement, and putting your marriage first. If you nail those, you should be solid. If any of them are seriously out of place, it could be a major problem.

#8431. Florida Man Finishes Four Corners Walk Across America

After over three years of trekking 20,000km back and forth across the continental United States, endurance athlete Kyndal Ray Edwards has completed his ambitious Four Corners Walk. His route took him from coast to coast, then corner to corner, starting in Florida and ending in Maine, where a crowd of supporters met him last week.

Kyndal Ray Edwards, 33, is originally from Orange Park, Florida. After struggling with drug addiction, mental illness, and the justice system during his early adulthood, he decided to become an endurance athlete and mental health advocate. On January 1, 2022, he began his walking project from the beach in Jacksonville.

Over the next 15 months, Edwards walked to Long Beach, Washington, traveling 5,298km from the Atlantic to the Pacific. His plans expanded, and he undertook the challenge of walking to all four corners of the contiguous United States. From Long Beach, he headed to the northwesternmost point, Cape Flattery, also in Washington. After trekking south to San Diego, the next corner he headed for was all the way back in Florida, the southeast extremity.

Along the way, Edwards posted frequently on social media about his journey and his recovery from mental illness.

On October 25, Edwards reached West Quoddy Head Lighthouse in Lubec, Maine. This is the northeast corner of the U.S., and the finish line for Edwards. He walked a total of 19,544 kilometers in 1,022 days, averaging nearly 20km per day.

Edwards and supporters at the finish line. Photo: Kyndal Ray Edwards

A cross-country walking journey, Edwards says, has been his dream since 2013. But with the end of his Walking Across America project, Edwards is already ruminating on future challenges. Online, he has proposed going on an adventure with a stranger in all 50 states, running more ultra marathons, and undertaking endurance challenges on every continent. However, he has yet to commit to one.

The end of Kyndal Ray Edwards’s journey came only days before Karl Bushby, another long-distance trekker, completed his 28-year Goliath Expedition. The pair also happen, Edwards pointed out, to share a birthday.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8429. Adventure Links of the Week

When we’re not outdoors, we get our adventure fix by exploring social media and the web. Here are some of the best adventure links we’ve discovered this week.

Yosemite Visitors Join Forces To Perform Extraordinary Rescue on Half Dome: When a Polish hiker slipped while descending the cables on Yosemite’s Half Dome, she slid down the steep granite face and was left clinging to the rock. Her fiancé then lost his footing while trying to reach her.

A group of strangers came together to rescue the pair. Using ropes, slings, carabiners, and climbing harnesses, the group formed a human chain to reach both stranded hikers and pull them to safety.

The Electric Arms Race Has Gone Too Far: Tim Shuff questions the growing use of electric motors in outdoor adventure. He argues that the speed and convenience they create undermine traditionally self-propelled activities like paddling and cycling. Today, you can even buy battery-powered SUPs.

While recognizing that electric assistance can make outdoor sports more accessible, he thinks that its wider use will change wilderness areas, reshape trails, and create pressure to build increasingly powerful equipment.

Jan Spicka descends from Huascarán Sur. Photo: Matej Zak

A Czech Alpinist Describes How He Survived a Nightmare on Peru’s Highest Mountain: Climbing Peru’s Huascarán Sur turned into a tragedy for a group of five friends last July. Czech alpinist Jan Spicka describes how he and partner Matej Zak reached the 6,768m summit before he plunged into a hidden crevasse on the descent.

Matej’s quick reactions saved his life, but their three friends, Saul Mendoza, Chicho Ugarte, and Arti Salas, disappeared while attempting to descend by a different route. With no official mountain rescue in Peru, friends and volunteers pitched in. After 24 hours in the freezing conditions, they knew it was unlikely they would see their friends again. GPS tracks and later searches suggest they had fallen through a collapsing snow bridge.

He Felt Stuck in New York. So He Started Walking to California: Feeling trapped by unemployment and a lack of opportunities in New York, 29-year-old Chantz Rivera began walking across America. He set off with a tent, sleeping bag, and some supplies in a shopping cart. Over 149 days, he walked to California, pushing his cart 32 to 40km a day along highways and through small towns, documenting his progress for his growing number of online followers.

This Mountain Race Makes Runners Carry 60kg of Beer and Supplies Uphill: Slovakia’s annual Sherpa Rallye is one of the world’s unique mountain races. Competitors have to haul heavy loads of supplies up a 5.7km rocky trail to a remote hut in the High Tatras, nearly 2,000m above sea level.

Men carry 60kg, including a 30-liter keg of beer, while women carry 20kg, with the loads secured to wooden frames. The race was started in 1985 by mountain porter Viktor Beranek. The event brings together the region’s nosiči (traditional mountain porters) and also helps stock the remote hut ahead of winter.

A Hiker Slid Hundreds of Meters Down Mount Timpanogos: A female hiker was seriously injured after slipping off the trail near the summit of Mount Timpanogos in Utah. She slid several hundred feet down the steep mountainside before landing on a narrow cliff ledge. Newly released helicopter footage shows her being hoisted to safety. She was taken to hospital and is reportedly recovering.

We Need to Move Past the ‘Shut Up and Die Like a Man’ Mentality in the Mountains: Climber and paraglider Will Gadd reflects on the psychological impact of mountain accidents. Climbing culture often glorifies the dangers people face in the mountains while discouraging them from admitting they are struggling.

Gadd discusses the deaths of two close friends and challenges the underlying expectation that climbers should simply suck it up and carry on. After decades in the mountains, he has seen that the consequences of an accident extend far beyond the immediate incident, affecting survivors and their families and friends for years.

Two Hikers Dead in Bear Attack in British Columbia: Two hikers, identified as 48-year-old Jennifer Balcarras and her 20-year-old daughter Selah Reed, have died in a suspected grizzly attack near the Snooka West Trail in Bella Coola, British Columbia. Their bodies were found during a search after the pair were reported missing by their family.

This comes just a year after a separate grizzly attack in the same area severely injured four people, including three children. The trail remains closed as authorities continue their investigation.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8428. Baselane Review: How to Stop Landlord Burnout From Costing You $800,000+

Rental property is how I broke free. The rent from my San Francisco properties was one of the foundations that let me leave banking in 2012 at age 34. Without it, I’d probably still be waking up at 5 am to get to the office by 5:45 am and leaving after 6 pm.

I’ve been a landlord since 2005. I’ve had good tenants, great tenants, and tenants who refused to take their trash with them during the final walkthrough.

I’m forever grateful for owning rental property. But the older I get, the less I enjoy being a landlord.

It’s not the math. It’s the tenant headaches, the turnover, and the maintenance. The 9 pm leaky faucet text. The final walkthrough that starts 30 minutes late. At 49 with two young kids, my time is worth more and my patience is worth less.

2025 tested me with three tenant turnovers. Each one meant a deposit to return, walls to paint, a vacancy to absorb, and a new tenant to find. Not long after, I sold one of my rentals. Earning 100% passive income feels better.

But selling also feels like giving up. Owning rental property is a war of attrition. The longer you hold, the more wealth you usually build. Hold until you pass, and your heirs may get a step-up in basis that wipes out the capital gains entirely.

Selling isn’t free either. Commissions, transfer taxes, capital gains, and depreciation recapture take a big bite. Then you lose the rent check and future appreciation on a property you’ll likely never buy back at the same price.

Semi-passive income you can live with beats optimized income you can’t wait to dump. You can’t automate away a difficult tenant or a burst pipe. But you can automate a lot of what surrounds them. That’s where Baselane comes in.

For 21 years, I’ve tracked my rental finances with a spreadsheet and emails. Old school. With three rentals left in San Francisco, it kind of works. I know my numbers, more or less.

But add another property, an LLC, a partner, or a short-term rental, and a DIY system starts to creak. Debits and credits slip through the cracks.

Four rentals is why I sold one last year. I couldn’t comfortably handle all the maintenance and management myself. And I didn’t want to pay a property manager 10% of rent or more to do a worse job than me.

Since Baselane is sponsoring this post, I opened a free account for one of my SF rentals to see how it compares. Here’s what stood out.

Baselane is a free business bank account, bookkeeping software, and rent collection app rolled into one login, built for landlords. Think business checking plus QuickBooks plus Venmo for rent, except it knows what a Schedule E is.

Banking and books live in the same system, so you never reconcile. Baselane is a fintech, not a bank. Deposits are held at Thread Bank, Member FDIC.

One of the first rules of landlording is to keep rental money separate from personal money. One of the second rules is that it gets messy fast once you own more than one property.

With Baselane, you can open unlimited checking and savings accounts under one login. One per property, one per LLC, one for reserves, however you actually run your portfolio. There are no monthly maintenance fees and no minimum balances, which matters once you're running a handful of accounts.

You can also issue physical and virtual debit cards with spending limits. If you pay contractors, cleaners, or a handyman, you can hand someone a card capped at, say, $1,500 for materials instead of fronting cash and chasing receipts.

The reserve accounts earn interest. Currently 2.88%. Letting a five-figure repair reserve sit in a checking account earning nothing is a donation to your bank.

If you try it, start with one property. Move its rent and its bills over, get comfortable, then decide about the rest.

Plenty of banks let you open separate accounts. What's different here is that every transaction gets tagged to a property and a Schedule E category as it happens.

Here's why that matters. Before my old Marina tenants moved in, I spent about $2,500 and many hours fixing up the yard. They agreed to hire a gardener twice a month. They never did. Before they moved out, I paid my landscaper $1,400 including materials to restore it, plus two days of my own labor.

Those are deductible repair and maintenance expenses. But only if you record them against the right property and can find the receipts a year later. With one property, I can do that myself. With five, it becomes a part-time job.

On the Smart plan, you set rules once, like “payments to my landscaper go to the SF rental under Repairs,” and an AI assistant handles the rest. You can snap a photo of a receipt and it matches it to the transaction.

This is the part that speaks to my real pain point: tenant headaches. It is also my favorite reason for using Baselane.

I rented my Marina house to five tenants from 2015 to 2017 for about $8,200 a month. The lease charged a $250 late fee after the 4th. Rent was late eight times in 24 months. I waived the fee every single time.

That’s $2,000 I left on the table because I’m a softie who dislikes confrontation. Their method of paying was walking into my bank branch and depositing directly into my account. One July 4th weekend, rent showed up on the 10th. That’s difficult to deal with when my mortgage is due by the 5th of each month, resulting in cash flow issues.

That awkwardness wore me down. In 2017, I sold the Marina house for about $2.75 million to simplify my life.

Thanks to the AI boom pushing San Francisco prices higher, that house is likely worth $800,000 to $1 million more today. Sure, I reinvested the proceeds, so the true opportunity cost is lower. But it still stings.

If I’d had a system to collect rent, send reminders, and enforce late fees without me playing bad cop, I would have held on. Selling out of fatigue can be one of the most expensive decisions a landlord makes.

That’s what Baselane does. Invoices, reminders, and late fees go out on schedule. Tenants can pay by ACH or card and set up autopay. Software doesn’t feel guilty enforcing a lease you both signed. Ultimately, Baselane removes friction which increases a rental property owner’s ability to hold on and build more wealth.

After 21 years, I've also learned that 99% of tenants want as little contact with their landlord as possible. The feeling is mutual. A system that runs rent in the background means fewer awkward conversations, and fewer awkward conversations means a happier landlord who's less tempted to sell.

Two catches. Tenants who pay by card pay a 3.49% fee, so steer them to ACH. And if your current rent collection works fine, then great. Baselane's value is rent, banking, and books living in one place.

If you run short-term rentals, payouts from Airbnb, Vrbo, or Booking.com can take several business days to clear at a normal bank. Across multiple listings, that's a constant gap between money earned and money available.

Baselane offers faster payouts when you use it as your deposit account on booking platforms, with each listing feeding its own account. Next-day payouts are available for free users.

A healthy bank balance doesn't mean a property is performing. You might have a big repair coming, insurance up 20%, or expenses sitting in the wrong account.

I recently ran the numbers on my San Francisco rental for a post on whether to raise the rent when tenants strike it rich. Rent is $10,250 a month, roughly $1,250 below market, because I honor a three-year rent schedule capped at about 3% a year. One vacant month wipes out about eight months of that discount. And a 2.5% annual increase only covers about 80% of my rising insurance and basic maintenance costs.

I know those numbers because I track them myself. Most landlords never sit down and run them.

Baselane builds income, expense, cash flow, and net operating income reports for each property or your whole portfolio, because every transaction is already tagged. No building spreadsheets from scratch.

With one rental and a spreadsheet you trust, you may not need this. With several properties or LLCs, it's the difference between knowing and guessing whether a property is worth keeping.

Tax season is where sloppy books come back to bite. You end up gathering records, digging up receipts, and trying to remember what a charge from eight months ago was for.

Because Baselane categorizes everything during the year, you can download a tax package with Schedule E reports, income statements, and transaction ledgers with receipts for each property and entity. Then you hand it to your CPA or use it to file yourself.

If you have one rental and a DIY system that works, you don't need it. But the Core plan is free, so there's little downside to trying it.

Baselane makes the most sense if you own several rentals, hold properties in LLCs, or self-manage. Property managers have quoted me fees ranging from 10% to 20% of rent. On a $10,250 rental, a 10% fee is $1,250 a month. Baselane Smart, its premium version is just $20.

That's not apples to apples. Baselane won't fix your garbage disposal or talk to your tenants. But if you self-manage, the administrative half of the job gets a lot lighter.

It's also worth a look if you're on the fence about selling a rental because you're worn out. Lighten the load before you list it and pay all those exorbitant fees. And if you're buying your next property, set up clean systems first. They're easier to build with one property than to retrofit with five.

Core is free and includes unlimited accounts, debit cards with spend controls, rent collection with automated reminders and late fees, property-level tracking, and the accountant tax package.

Smart is $20 a month with a 30-day free trial and adds AI auto-tagging, receipt matching, auto-transfers to maintain target balances, faster rent deposits, and balance sheet reports.

Compare that to what you may already pay. Some business bank accounts charge $5 to $25 a month in maintenance fees. At $15 a month across five accounts, that's $900 a year just to keep them open. Baselane charges no maintenance fees, no minimums, and ACH transfers and incoming wires are free.

No product is perfect, and pretending otherwise insults your intelligence.

Rental property helped me break free due to the semi-passive income it generates. But a rental only builds wealth if you can stand to keep owning it.

The headaches that make me want to sell are tenants, turnover, and maintenance. Baselane doesn't make those entirely disappear. What it does is take the money side of landlording, rent collection, late fees, accounts, and records, and put it on autopilot. Less friction overall means more energy for the parts that actually need a human.

My plan going forward is simple. Own fewer, better rentals and make each one as close to passive as possible. Let the private real estate side of my portfolio be 100% passive. And spend the time I save being a full-time dad.

If I’d had automated rent and late fees with my Marina tenants, I’d have $2,000 more and far fewer awkward conversations. I also might still own the house today. That’s at least an $800,000 lesson in letting small annoyances drive big decisions.

Selling a rental is permanent. Making one easier to own isn't.

If you own rental property and want it to feel a little more passive, check out Baselane here. The Core plan is free, so the only cost of trying it is an afternoon.

Have you ever sold a rental property because you were tired of managing it, not because the numbers stopped working? Do you regret it? What's the one landlord headache that would push you to sell: tenants, turnover, maintenance, or the paperwork? Do you track your rental finances yourself or hand them off? And have you ever waived a late fee you were fully entitled to collect?

What should real estate investors look for when choosing a bank? Look for property-specific accounts, integrated rent collection, automated bookkeeping, and tax prep support. Multi-property investors benefit most from platforms that organize transactions by property and entity automatically. Security deposit tracking also helps keep tenant funds clearly separated.

Can real estate investors open a business bank account online? Yes. Digital platforms like Baselane let you open accounts entirely online with no branch visit. Traditional banks may require in-person verification depending on their policies and your business structure.

Do any banking platforms for real estate investors offer high-yield savings? Digital-first platforms generally pay more on savings than traditional banks. Baselane pays a tiered APY on savings balances that exceeds the national average for business accounts. Traditional banks typically pay far less on business savings.

How do integrated banking and bookkeeping platforms help with taxes? Platforms like Baselane categorize transactions by property and IRS Schedule E category throughout the year. That cuts manual work at tax time and produces Schedule E reports and transaction ledgers you can hand to your accountant or use to file yourself.

Does Baselane support multiple properties? Yes. Baselane supports bookkeeping across multiple rental properties for U.S.-based entities, including single-member and multi-member LLCs, corporations, partnerships, and sole proprietorships. Banking and rent collection sync directly with bookkeeping, and you can connect external bank accounts and import historical transactions. See the pricing page for the full feature list.

What reports can real estate investors generate? Portfolio-level and property-level reports including net operating income, net cash flow, rent roll, tenant ledger, total inflows and outflows, transaction ledger with receipts, balance sheet, Schedule E, and income statement. Reports export for your accountant, lender, or tax filing.

Been a landlord for 17 years. Thankfully, the rent coming in is the easy part as I screen my tenants very carefully and charge below market rent.

It’s everything else that gets old. The paperwork, tracking expenses, chasing contractors, dealing with insurance renewals, etc. Anything that cuts down on administrative work is a win in my book.

I’ve been a landlord for over 20 years and couldn’t agree more with your point about the emotional toll of managing rental properties. People always focus on appreciation, cash flow, and tax benefits, but rarely account for the time and mental energy involved.

I’ve had some wonderful tenants, but a few difficult ones can really change your perspective. At this stage, I’d gladly sacrifice a little cash flow for less hassle. The trick is remembering that owning real estate should improve your life, not become your second job.

#8426. Antarctica 2026-2027: Expedition Roundup

Here are the Antarctic expeditions for 2026-27. There will be more, as a few still scramble to raise the money or choose to keep their plans to themselves until the last minute. Because of the long charter flights, an expedition to Antarctica, even a “tourist” Last Degree trip, costs close to $100,000 — more, if you want to do something unusual. These are the journeys announced so far. Skiers hit the ice later this month.

Hercules Inlet is the common starting point for South Pole skiers. Skiers need to travel around 1,130km, averaging around 20km per day.

Brit Nathan Lumb originally planned a fully unsupported round-trip to the South Pole but scaled back due to “expedition logistics and funding realities.” He will now ski solo and unsupported from Hercules Inlet to the South Pole. Lumb previously completed a crossing of Norway’s Finnmarksvidda with guiding company Ousland Colliard.

Indian Neeraj Choudhary also trained with Ousland Colliard, crossing the Greenland Ice Sheet in 2024. He hopes to become the second Indian to ski solo and unsupported to the South Pole, following Satish Gogineni, who became the first in 2024-2025.

Satish Gogineni at the South Pole in 2025. Photo: Satish Gogineni

American Lucas Czarnecki will also ski solo and unsupported and claims he will livestream every moment of his journey: “What sets South Signal [his expedition name] apart from other polar expeditions is my commitment to bringing you along for every moment of this extreme adventure. Each day, I will be live-streaming my experience directly from the ice, sharing the raw reality of life on the polar plateau.”

Another American, Meghan Buchanan, will also tackle the route. She plans to start early, leaving for Antarctica on November 12.

Finally, German Dirk Langer will also attempt the route unsupported.

A Berkner Island start means a few extra kilometers, with skiers required to cover around 1,400km to the South Pole.

Max Worsley of the UK and Martin Nesse of Norway also plan to start in early November, but from the edge of Berkner Island. Max is the son of Henry Worsley, who died following an Antarctic expedition in 2015-2016. Max intends to follow much of his father’s final route.

At least two expeditions will begin at the Messner start, 911km from the South Pole. James Garnett of the UK and Norwegian Marius Rehn will each ski solo and unsupported.

Satish Gogineni will be back in Antarctica this year, hoping to build upon his Hercules Inlet experience. Gogineni’s 2026 plan is more ambitious: a solo, 2,000km journey from the outer edge of the Filchner–Ronne Ice Shelf to the inner edge of the Ross Ice Shelf via the South Pole. Gogineni is billing this as a “world-first human achievement” and the “longest solo traverse ever completed on the Antarctic continent.”

If successful, his route would indeed be longer than the truncated crossings of Colin O’Brady and Lou Rudd in 2018. However, his distance would still be well short of Borge Ousland’s 1997 expedition. Ousland became the first person to cross Antarctica solo and unsupported, though he occasionally used a rudimentary handheld kite known as a ski sail. Ousland skied for 65 days and covered 2,845km. At the time, sails were not considered “support.”

Last year, O’Brady tried a significantly longer expedition than his 2018 effort, attempting to ski from the edge of the Ross Ice Shelf to the tip of Berkner Island on the edge of the Ronne Ice Shelf, a total distance of over 2,800km. He eventually ended his crossing attempt after 100 days, with roughly 40% of his total distance remaining.

A map by PECS (the Polar Expeditions Classification Scheme) showing previous solo Antarctic crossing routes. Photo: PECS

Gogineni’s main challenge might be the weight of his sled. With so much weight spread over two sleds, O’Brady was forced to double carry in soft snow on the Ross Ice Shelf last year. A double carry involved shuttling half of his gear forward, then returning to pick up the rest. This meant he had to cover 15km for every 5km of progress. Gogineni says his sled will weigh 181kg.

Mardi Philips, Ida Olsson, and Nia Owens are calling themselves the Antarctic Valkyries and aim to become the “first all-female team to reach the South Pole of Inaccessibility.” They will start at Thorshammer Station (a spot near the Russian Novolazarevskaya Station in Queen Maud Land) and plan to kite-ski 2,500km, first aiming for the Pole of Inaccessibility (the middle of the Antarctic continent), and then continuing to their finish point at the South Pole.

The Antarctic Valkyries proposed route. Photo: Antarctic Valkyries

A Spanish scientific expedition will cross Antarctica by a new route in a unique wind-powered vehicle.

Part of the wind-sled involves a series of tents. Photo: La Vanguardia

Their “wind-sled ” is 15m long and consists of four modules. It is propelled using 20 kites of various sizes. The largest kite measures 150m² and the smallest 5m². The four-man team plans a 3,400km, 85-day journey from near Novolazarevskaya Station to the Italian research station Concordia, known as Dome C, on the Antarctic Plateau.

The wind-sled team’s proposed route. The green return route is planned for a future expedition. Photo: La Vanguardia

Meanwhile, three Argentine kayakers are preparing to paddle roughly 900km along the western coast of the Antarctic Peninsula, from Argentina’s Esperanza Base to San Martín Base. Diego Linares, Juan Tomaselli, and Javier Siede will start their journey in January and plan to spend 30 to 40 days on the water.

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

#8424. A Risky Summit Push on Everest

It’s now or never for the Russian team on the West Ridge of Everest. Andrey Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov have set off for the summit without oxygen, hoping the weather will hold. They are completely alone on the mountain, and the push is full of risk.

Everest is still loaded with fresh snow after two cyclones, and there are only three of them to break trail and find the best route above 7,500m. That was their highest point during their earlier venture in October.  In addition, the wind may rise dramatically on Thursday, Vitor Baia, a Portuguese high-altitude meteorologist, told ExplorersWeb.

“It looks like we have a weather window of about a week, followed by a significant intensification of the wind,” leader Andrei Vasiliev told Anna Piunova of Mountain Club.

The question is exactly when the wind will increase, and whether the climbers will have enough time to summit and descend to a safer altitude.

“They have three days of weak wind, until Wednesday [November 11], but then the wind will increase sharply,” said Baia.

He says the best summit day will be Tuesday, with clear skies and weak winds. “Then they could use Wednesday to descend,” he added. “[That day will have] some clouds but still acceptable wind speeds.”

The situation will worsen remarkably on Thursday, when the wind will shift from the west to the northwest and increase dramatically to 50 kph at 8,000m and 60kph at the summit.

In addition, avalanche hazards are still high due to the large amounts of snow after the latest storm two weeks ago. Finally, as winter approaches, it’s getting colder every day, the climbers reported.

After their first trip up ended in a tactical retreat as the cyclone hit, they retreated to Kathmandu to recover from some health issues. Now they are fit again and ready for a second try. They returned to Base Camp last Thursday. Not surprisingly, they found it covered in a thick new layer of snow. Breaking trail, they sank at some points up to their waists, Vasiliev reported.

“Our high-altitude boots were in a cache two hours ahead of us, so we used duct tape to seal our pant cuffs to our trekking boots, trying to keep the snow out,” Kondrashkin reported. They eventually reached the cache and continued up in an exhausting 14-hour-long effort to a second cache (their Camp 1), where they had left their tent and sleeping bags.

They were near an avalanche-prone area and several snow slides swept nearby, prompting them to wait a day before continuing. Meanwhile, Shipilov showed signs of food poisoning. Kodrashkin recalls that Saturday morning started with the following conversation:

“Vitalya, how are you feeling? Are we heading up?”

That answer needs some clarification. A professional like Vitaly Viktorovich [Shipilov] really only has two modes: “We’re going” and “I’m dying.”

In fact, Shipilov had a fever and acute pain in the lower back, so the team headed down to their base in Lobuche. As this was going on, the snow had time to sluff off from the previously avalanche-prone area.

After just one night of rest, Vasiliev confirmed they were going up again. If all went according to plan, they will spend tonight back in their tent above the Lho La. Unfortunately, the climbers have not shared their tracker showing their current location and progress. On their previous trip up, they reached some 7,500m on Everest’s West Shoulder, near the beginning of the West Ridge and the potential detour to the Hornbein Couloir.

Everest’s North Face from the base of the West Ridge. Photo: Sergey Kondrashkin

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

#8421. An Internal Locus Of Control Makes You A Happier, Wealthier Investor

An internal locus of control is the belief that you control your outcomes and can solve problems as they come up. An external locus of control is the belief that outside forces run your life. Luck. The economy. Your boss. Some CEO you’ve never met.

Peter Gray, a research psychologist at Boston College argues in his new book, Restoring Childhood, that kids have become more anxious and depressed over the decades because adults took away their free play and independence. Children who never get to solve their own problems never build an internal locus of control.

And psychologists have long known that a weak internal locus of control is strongly linked to anxiety and depression.

It makes sense. If you believe you have little control over what happens to you, the world feels cruel. At the extreme, you feel helpless. And when you feel helpless, darker thoughts start creeping in.

As a father of young children, this hit home. As an investor, it hit my wallet. Recently, I got a $50,000 lesson in what it feels like to have zero control.

From an investor’s point of view, the active investor has an internal locus of control. He believes his research and decisions can beat the market.

The passive investor has more of an external locus of control. He accepts that he can’t beat the market, so he buys the whole thing and lets it ride.

The irony is, despite the better feeling of being in control, most active investors underperform over time. I agree with that, which is why about 60% to 70% of my public investments are in the S&P 500 and the Nasdaq.

But I have two problems with fully going passive.

First, I don’t want to earn the same returns as everybody else. I want to outperform so my family’s buying power grows faster than the masses. If not, we'll just be running in place with every other passive index investor.

Second, accepting that I have no control makes me less satisfied. I want to believe my decisions matter. So I keep a chunk of my portfolio in individual stocks. One of them was Nvidia for the past several years.

My Nvidia position was worth about $150,000 in October 2026. It’s now worth about $100,000.

About $30,000 of the decline came from the broader AI selloff. Fine. That’s the market. I can live with that.

First he admitted to an affair with Alice Huang, an executive at Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer. U.S. officials reportedly believe she was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Then the New York Times reported a second relationship, this time with an Nvidia executive who reported to him. Days later, Jensen resigned as the board moved to oust him. The stock dropped again.

Finding and keeping love truly is harder than getting rich.

Losing $50,000 is about a year and a half of food for my family of four. It’s also about a year and a half of the unsubsidized health insurance premiums we pay.

As the household’s provider, I feel like a failure for blowing up so much money. Without a steady day job, our investment returns are what keep us going. Every loss hits harder.

I’m also annoyed at Jensen. I didn’t sign up for this as a shareholder. I naively thought someone that disciplined would keep business and pleasure separate.

But mostly, I’m annoyed with myself for putting too much capital in one company.

Here’s the uncomfortable part. Blaming Jensen is the external locus of control response.

An external locus of control means believing outside forces decide your fate. When I say “Jensen cost me $20,000,” I’m pinning my loss on a guy I’ve never met and can’t control.

It may be true. But it leaves me helpless. I can’t stop the next CEO from falling for someone he shouldn’t. I can’t make a board build a succession plan. And I definitely can’t make billionaires stay faithful.

So the internal locus of control question is: what could I have controlled?

Plenty, it turns out. I chose the position size. I chose to put about 40% of my public portfolio in individual stocks, instead of a more conservative 20% – 30% as suggested in my article, the Recommended Split Between Passive And Active Investing. I broke my own rule.

Jensen made the mess. I decided how much it could cost me.

I also never priced in key man risk. Jensen ran Nvidia for 33 years and the company had no successor ready. When one person is the company, his personal life is part of your investment. I need to bake that into my model.

And here’s the kicker. Going passive wouldn’t have 100% saved me. Nvidia is one of the largest holdings in both the S&P 500 and the Nasdaq. My index funds got dinged too. Passive investing doesn’t remove the Jensen risk. It only dilutes it.

Since the Fed started aggressively hiking rates in 2022, real estate has gotten little love. Mortgage rates follow the 10-year Treasury yield, which shot up, and demand softened across the country. Meanwhile, the S&P 500, especially tech and AI stocks, grabbed the spotlight.

This Nvidia debacle reminded me why I prefer owning real estate over stocks. Living in your primary residence and having it appreciate it the background is easy.

As a landlord, I have a high internal locus of control. I choose the tenants. I decide when to fix the roof, remodel the kitchen or replace the carpet. I set rents within the law. If the property underperforms, it’s usually on me.

And because I can make it better, I feel more confident and happier owning it.

No CEO can tank the value of my house with a bad decision in his personal life.

Real estate still has risks. Insurance covers the physical ones like fire and water damage, and with separate policies, earthquakes and floods. But insurance won’t protect you from a local recession, a spike in mortgage rates or a shift in rent laws.

Your property can absolutely lose value. It just won’t drop 20% in a week because one person made a bad decision.

Sure, real estate may only appreciate 3% to 5% a year compared to roughly 10% for the S&P 500. But with 20% down, 4% appreciation is a 20% return on your equity before costs.

Plus you collect rent every month, and that rent doesn’t shrink the property’s value the way a dividend shrinks a company’s cash pile. In this regard, rental income is superior to dividend income.

The older I get, the more I value steady over spectacular. I’d rather not watch $20,000 vanish in a few days.

Here’s the funny thing. Say it turned out I was the International Personal Finance Playboy of the World, with multiple girlfriends and a dozen kids like Elon Musk. As an Asian American, I'd be crucified and it would be a scandal in the personal finance community.

If Financial Samurai were a publicly traded penny stock, the shares might drop. Then again, bad PR is still PR. Some media outlets might cover it, traffic would surge and the stock would probably go up.

But my real estate wouldn’t care either way. However bad a husband or father I might be, my properties won’t lose value because of my behavior. As an an investor, that's a beautiful thing.

The only ways my real estate goes down are if the local economy turns or I neglect the properties and stop keeping tenants happy and rents at market. Both are mostly in my control.

So real estate remains my favorite asset class for building wealth. It provides necessary shelter and generates a lot more income than dividends to help you break free from work sooner.

For the playboys and playgirls out there, it might be even more valuable.

Now for the contradiction. The older and wealthier I get, the less I want to be the landlord dealing with leaky toilets and tenant turnovers. I’ve been slowly selling physical rentals and reinvesting in private real estate for more passive income.

Doesn’t that mean giving up control? Yes.

But I’ve realized control was never the whole point. What I really want to avoid is single-person risk. A diversified pool of rental homes can't be taken down by a single person. One bad tenant or one bad house doesn’t sink it. You still have to trust the fund manager, so do your homework there too.

The healthiest locus of control is neither fully internal nor fully external. Control what you can: your savings rate, your asset allocation, your position sizes and the properties you choose to own. Then accept what you can’t, without feeling helpless.

Peter Gray’s point is that kids build an internal locus of control by doing hard things on their own: playing, exploring, failing and figuring it out without an adult hovering.

Investing works the same way. You build confidence by making decisions, owning the results and adjusting. Losing $50,000 in Nvidia this quickly stings. But if it forces me to size positions better, it’ll make me a better investor over time.

Kids learn the same lesson in a different way. That’s one reason I want mine helping with tenant turnovers and rental property maintenance. Patching a wall, helping me do landscaping projects, and seeing a rental get leased teaches them that their effort changes outcomes.

Sometimes it’s nice to outsource your financial and parenting duties to competent people. Just make sure they’re actually competent.

The last thing you want is to pay someone for worse results than you could have gotten yourself.

How much of your portfolio is in individual stocks, and have you ever lost big because of one executive’s behavior? Does owning real estate make you feel more in control, or more stressed? Do you factor key man risk into founder-led companies? And for the parents out there, are you giving your kids enough room to develop an internal locus of control?

AI may be the next general-purpose technology, but compute needs physical buildings and power. One way to get exposure without owning a single chip stock is through private real estate and venture.

Check out Fundrise, which invests in residential and industrial real estate and private AI companies. No CEO scandal can sink a diversified portfolio of properties. Fundrise is a long-time sponsor of Financial Samurai.

Sign up for the private Financial Samurai newsletter!

I really enjoyed this perspective. I think it really is helpful to practice distinguishing between what we can control and what we can’t and have to accept or pivot. Investing always involves uncertainty and even the best research and intentions can’t protect us from someone else’s decisions or unexpected events.

But we can control our diversification, risk exposure, and how we respond when things don’t go our way. I also appreciate the connection to parenting, too. Building confidence comes from having the freedom to make decisions, experience setbacks, and learn from them. Ultimately, it’s not about controlling every outcome but having confidence in our ability to adapt and make better decisions along the way. Thanks for sharing such a thoughtful and relatable reminder

In this reflective personal finance essay, Financial Samurai uses a $50,000 loss on Nvidia stock—triggered in part by a fictionalized 2026 executive scandal—to illustrate the psychological distinction between an internal and external locus of control. Rather than blaming corporate leadership, the author emphasizes controlling position sizing and key-man risk while highlighting why diversified real estate offers greater emotional stability and insulation from single-person failures.

#8417. Deciding Between A Dream House And Millions In Anthropic Stock

With Anthropic going public at a $1.5 trillion valuation, a lot of people in San Francisco are about to face a decision they have never faced before. Further, they're experiencing FOMO or regret in not investing more in Anthropic earlier.

So let's run a fun thought experiment for an “everyday millionaire” in San Francisco. You get to pick one, and only one:

Option 1: A $7.5 million, 4,500 square foot, fully remodeled 5 bedroom / 5 bathroom single-family home on a large lot with views in San Francisco. Free and clear. Move in tomorrow.

Option 2: $8.5 million of Anthropic stock at whatever it prices at, with a six-month lockup.

My first goal is to set the line like a bookie would, splitting readers as close to 50/50 as I can.

My second goal is to get you thinking about what the money is actually for. Follow the chain backwards. You take the job for the equity and hope there's huge upside. You want the windfall to buy a house. And you want the house because that’s where your family lives.

Option 1 hands you the end of that chain today. No lockup, no tax bill, no waiting. That is the whole debate.

You probably noticed Option 2 is worth more on paper. That is not a thumb on the scale. It is the tax code.

A house and a share of stock are not the same currency. One is an after-tax asset that throws off shelter every single day. The other is a pre-tax asset that throws off nothing at all.

To convert Anthropic stock into a $7.5 million house in California, you need $11.92 million of stock at long-term capital gains rates. That is 20% federal, plus the 3.8% net investment income tax, plus 13.3% to California. A 37.1% haircut.

And that is the friendly version. If your shares are RSUs that vest at the IPO, they get taxed as ordinary income. Now you need $15.84 million of stock to buy the same house.

Quick note for early employees: qualified small business stock could wipe out a chunk of that federal bill. But Anthropic blew past the $50 million gross assets test years ago, so this only helps a very small number of people who got shares in 2021.

So a $7.5 million house is worth roughly $11.9 million of Anthropic stock today. I set the line at $8.5 million because the stock also gets four years to grow, and the house does not grow nearly as fast.

I have a long-standing belief that equity in a company is a type of funny money. The value is based on growth and profit assumptions years into the future. A stock provides no utility. Its value can tank overnight, like we've seen with NVIDIA and many tech stocks recently. You can look at it on a screen, but that's about it.

Only when you sell it and buy something does it become real. And the moment you sell it, the government takes a third or more.

A house is different. A house pays you every single day in the form of shelter you do not have to rent. Your goal in getting rich from stock is to buy the dream house, not the other way around.

Owning that $7.5 million home outright costs $117,750 a year, and property taxes are almost the whole story at $87,750. Utilities, gardening, and services run $15,000. Maintenance on a freshly remodeled house is about $10,000. Insurance is $5,000. So “free and clear” still costs you $9,812 a month before you buy a single grocery.

But renting the same house in San Francisco costs about $25,000 a month, a 4% gross yield. So owning it beats renting it by $182,250 a year, and that gap grows. Over the six-month lockup alone, that is $91,125 handed to a landlord while you wait for permission to sell.

Proposition 13. Your assessment is locked at your purchase price and can only rise 2% a year. If the house doubles, your tax bill does not. Meanwhile your stock's tax bill grows right alongside the gain.

The $500,000 exclusion. A married couple shields half a million of gain when they sell a primary residence. Anthropic stock gets zero exclusion.

You can borrow against it without triggering a taxable event. Try getting a line of credit against locked-up pre-IPO shares.

And there is the part that does not show up in a spreadsheet. Your kids get bedrooms. You might get a rare enclosed front yard, or the sunset ocean view at 7pm. You cannot raise a family inside a brokerage account.

Now let me argue against myself, because the bull case is not stupid.

Anthropic's 2026 annualized revenue run rate is about $80 billion, up from roughly $47 billion in May and about $9 billion at the end of 2025. The company also reported its first positive adjusted operating income, reversing a loss of about $5.6 billion in 2024.

Here is my pro forma, and I am deliberately staying inside the company's own guidance. I have the run rate at $90 billion by December 2026, $150 billion by December 2027, and $200 billion by December 2028. Growth decelerates from 67% to 33%, which is still extraordinary for a business of that size.

If those numbers land, which I think they will because companies sandbag their first guidance, the multiple does the work for you. Whatever Anthropic prices at, that number gets cut roughly in half by the end of 2027 and in half again by the end of 2028. The company simply grows into the price.

A house is not going to do that. A house is going to sit there and appreciate at optimistically 10% per annum, but probably closer to 5%, while asking you for a new roof.

Say you take the $8.5 million in Anthropic stock, ride out the lockup, and plan to buy your dream house with an enclosed front yard in four years.

The house is not waiting for you. At 5% appreciation, $7.5 million becomes $9.1 million. And while you wait, you are renting at $25,000 a month, which burns about $1.27 million over four years.

So the stock has to produce $10.4 million after tax just to get you back to even. Pre-tax, that means your $8.5 million stock has to become $16.5 million. The extra $6.1 million goes to the government.

Anthropic has to appreciate 1.9 times, from wherever it prices, within four years. Call it a double. That is an 18.1% compound annual return.

If it prices at $1.4 trillion, you need $2.7 trillion. If it prices at $1.5 trillion, you need $2.9 trillion. The dollar figure moves. The requirement does not. The house costs what it costs, rent costs what it costs, and California takes what it takes. None of that cares what Goldman and Morgan Stanley settle on tonight.

A 50% gain is not enough. Up 50% leaves you with $8 million after tax, more than a million short of the asking price alone, and $2.4 million short once you count the rent you burned getting there.

If growth keeps decelerating, from 33% in 2028 to 25% in 2029 to 20% in 2030, the run rate reaches roughly $300 billion by the time the four years are up.

So the real question is what multiple of that $300 billion the market has to pay for you to break even.

Mature software companies trade at 6 to 10 times sales. Run the math across the range and there is a clean dividing line at about $1.54 trillion.

Price below that, and break-even only requires an ordinary multiple. At $1.4 trillion you need the market to pay 9.1 times sales in 2030. At $1.5 trillion, 9.7 times. Both sit inside the normal band. The stock wins even if Anthropic gets re-rated down to a perfectly boring software company.

Price above $1.54 trillion, and break-even requires a premium multiple that still has to be there four years from now. That is a different bet entirely, because it depends on sentiment rather than execution.

For perspective, back in September, when Anthropic was floating a $2 trillion valuation, break-even required 13 times sales.

SpaceX went public in June at around $1.75 trillion. It surged past $2 trillion, then fell back. It has been a volatile stock out of the gate, and Anthropic may trade the same way.

That is the thing about a six-month lockup. You do not get to sell into the euphoria. You get to watch it, and then you get to find out what is left when everybody else's lockup expires on the same day as yours.

The other half of this trade is that San Francisco real estate is in a melt-up, driven by the exact wealth we are discussing.

Between January and late June 2026, 136 single-family homes closed at or above $5 million, more than double the 67 transactions in the same period a year earlier. In June alone, 44 homes sold for at least $1 million over asking. At the very top, even all-cash offers of $25 million and up have been outbid.

This remodeled Victorian on Castro St. sold in September for $7,650,000 against a $5,495,000 list. That is 39% over asking, $2.15 million above what the sellers hoped for, for 3,416 square feet on a 2,622 square foot lot. Smaller than the house in our scenario. This is what the money actually buys here.

A 4,500 square foot remodeled home on a large lot with views is a unicorn property. Homes with views usually don’t have large useable lots and vice versa. Maybe 5-8 trade in a given year. If you wait four years to buy it with your stock proceeds, you are not bidding against today's buyers. You are bidding against every other newly liquid Anthropic and OpenAI employee who made the same plan you did.

Instead of the $7.5 million house being worth $9.1 million in four years, it could be worth well north of $10 million.

That is the trap. Everybody's exit strategy is the same few houses.

Some of you are already typing that the answer is to take the stock and move somewhere a mansion costs $2 million. Two problems.

The job that created the windfall is in San Francisco, so leaving means leaving the equity behind. And California taxes equity compensation based on where you worked while it vested, so moving after the fact does not get you out of the bill you are trying to avoid.

Further, would you really walk away from a job like that when there are so many more millions to make? Only if you’re a FIRE person who values time and freedom more than money.

As for the mansion itself, I toured a bunch of them in Honolulu last summer and came away thinking buying a mansion in paradise might be backwards. Then again, having a mansion in a beautiful city like San Francisco is a pretty amazing combination.

Real wealth is not the number. Real wealth is the gap between what you own and what you can actually enjoy.

You can spend the next four years watching a ticker and eating at home to preserve optionality, and end up with $10.4 million and four years of your kids' childhoods spent in a sub-optional rental you did not love. Or you can spend those four years in the dream house, providing for your family and making memories, and end up with $9.1 million or more of home equity plus four years you actually lived.

That said, I believe Anthropic could reach $3 trillion in market cap by the end of 2027 and $5 trillion by the end of 2028. Against anything in tonight's IPO price range, that is a healthy amount of upside. If I am right, the stock wins this debate within about thirteen months and it is not even a contest.

I drew the fair line myself, and I am still leaning toward the house. Watching names like Google and Nvidia drop 30% to 40% in a few months is a reminder of how fickle stocks are. And not having to fight a bidding war over a unicorn property would save my wife and me a lot of heartbreak.

At 49 with two young kids, time is too precious to put my life on hold for money that won't be used. I might end up with far more money holding Anthropic stock. But by the time I sell and pay the capital gains, I doubt I can lock down the dream house at a reasonable price.

Because if I have $20 million in Anthropic stock in four years, so do thousands of other employees and investors. Bidding against one buyer is hard enough. Bidding against dozens for the same unicorn property is impossible to win. And what good is money you cannot convert into the thing you wanted it for?

So I take the bird in hand. I have watched enough people optimize their way through a decade and arrive at the finish line wealthy and vaguely disappointed. Money is a means to an end. And one of those ends is living in the nicest house you can comfortably afford.

Readers, Which would you take: the $7.5 million dream house, or $8.5 million of Anthropic stock with a six-month lockup? And why? Note: All your comments eventually go through. The spam filter is just working overdrive. Thanks for your patience.

Most people reading this will not be deciding between a $7.5 million house and an IPO windfall. But the same AI boom driving both is also driving something more boring: the physical buildout underneath it. Every model needs compute, compute needs data centers, and data centers need land, power, and permits. That is real estate, and it is the constraint nobody can code their way around.

All of which is a long way of saying that you could take the money and have a much nicer life somewhere else. $2M will buy you a really nice house in most of the country outside of CA and NYC (and maybe a few other major metro areas), and with a much lower ongoing OPEX. With the rest of that fat stack, you could retire early and get off the hamster wheel, just as you did when you decided you were done and retired early.

Like it or not, the sacrifices required to keep living in the Bay Area are unsustainable for most of us. Even for those of us that can afford to live there, based on the silly cost of housing, the more rational choice is to move somewhere else.

With inflation, property taxes, and insurance costs all roaring, a very expensive home continues to look less and less appealing. This is sad of course, because real estate is what defines the “look and feel” of a place – but more and more, the cost just balloons with little actual value being added or created.

Inflation is actually great when you hold a real physical asset. It means its value is inflating as well. Inflation is worst for cash holders when intend to use their cash to buy something like a home. It loses its purchasing power as time goes on.

Owning shelter is fundamental and one of the best, if not the best inflation hedges there is.

This is of course, true. Inflation is a fantastic tool for generating wealth. However, when left unchecked, it can cause the dreaded “K-shaped” situation we are currently experiencing. So owning fantastic appreciating assets becomes less fantastic when everything around you is collapsing and there is chaos and unrest. I am 100% a capitalist, conservative – but it’s important to honestly acknowledge the pros and cons of this mechanism for everyone.

Yes, the inflation mechanism is real and can help create wealth. However, if it goes too far, it can lead to chaos, collapse, and ruin. I think we need to be aware of that. Conservatism isn’t just capitalism and free markets, but also conserving – preserving – a safe, orderly, good society. One people want to live in.

Honestly, I’d probably take the stock but wouldn’t buy either a $7.5 million house or immediately sell all the shares. I’d keep my existing lifestyle, diversify gradually after the lockup, and perhaps buy a much less expensive home.

The difference could fund decades of travel, family experiences, and freedom from having to work. A $7.5 million house sounds amazing, but so does never having to worry about money again.

I’d also be careful assuming the current revenue growth trajectory can continue indefinitely.

Even exceptional companies eventually face growth constraints. Personally, I’d want to diversify at least some of the windfall rather than have so much of my net worth tied to one company.

#8415. Winners of the Banff Mountain Film Festival Announced

The annual Banff Centre Mountain Festival gathers mountain luminaries from across the world at the Banff Centre for Arts and Creativity in Alberta. There, a celebration of mountain culture recognizes the best outdoor films and books of the past year. The climax of the festival took place this past weekend, and the winners of the 51st annual Banff Mountain Film Competition have now been announced.

The Grand Prize went to Listen, directed by Taliesin Black-Brown. In this short documentary, sound engineer Hank Lentfer records the sounds of nature around his Alaska home, while caring for his mother with dementia. As Lentfer documents the degradation of the natural world, we witness the slow loss of his mother. Drawing together these two threads, the film explores connection despite, and through, loss, using sound.

Rose Bush, an Oscar Award-winning director of photography serving on the competition’s jury, called Listen “a film that engages human-caused environmental devastation — the existential dilemma of our time — at its nucleus with overwhelming love and hope.”

The film also won the Grand Jury Prize at the Tribeca Film Festival.

In addition to a grand prize, the festival honors films in several categories. The winning film in the Adventure category was The Navigator, a feature-length work from director Jordan Manley. The film follows Nainoa Thompson, who was the first Native Hawaiian in six centuries to make the journey from Hawaii to Tahiti in a traditional voyaging canoe. Now, almost five decades later, Thompson and his crew make the journey again, using only traditional navigation techniques.

The best film in the Mountain Culture category was Land of Canaan, produced and directed by Maggie Lemere. Lemere’s film follows a group of Palestinian olive farmers, who work to protect their ancient olive groves amid the destruction by Israel. The film “illuminated the vital relationship of courage, vision, hope, and traditional ecological knowledge,” juror Rose Bush said, “in service of the essential value of sovereignty via mountain culture.”

In the Environment category, director James Dawson’s winning comedic documentary Derek vs Derek follows two Devon farmers, both named Derek. One Derek is an ardent dairy farmer while his environmentalist neighbor begins rewilding his land. A feud naturally ensues.

The Arc’teryx-sponsored Best Climbing Film was Zindabad, directed by Tom Fish. Fish follows Pakistani climber Mueez Ud Din into the Karakoram, alongside his uncle Javed Ali and French climber Mathieu Maynadier. Ud Din explores a remote, unknown glacier system, while the film examines what it means to be a Pakistani climber among peaks long claimed by international outsiders.

Directed by Alexis Berg, the Mountain Sports winner, Roots, follows trail runner Ruth Croft as she examines her future as an elite ultra-trail runner after an injury. Roots is “about an inner journey of healing and acceptance, rooted in the essential joy of running outdoors,” explained juror David Arnaud.

The Snow Sports winner centers on American skier Jessie Diggins. Diggins is the most decorated American cross-country skier ever, but Threshold, directed by Torsten Brinkema, reveals the deep personal struggles behind that success. Juror Beki Henderson noted that “the jury particularly commends Jessie Diggins for her extraordinary courage and honesty in sharing her story.”

There were more mountain films this year than you can even imagine. With 610 film submissions and 83 finalists, it’s no wonder that there are still more category winners.

Best short film winner Hello, Beautiful follows climber Alannah Yip as she struggles with identity, training for the Olympics while enduring medical hair loss. Director Darcy Hennessey created a film which “reminds us that personal transformation does not always come from pushing harder,” juror Kerry Stauffer explained.

For best feature film, the competition announced Ceremony, directed and produced by Banchi Hanuse. The film tells the story of the disappearance of the ooligan, a small fish, from British Columbia’s Bella Coola River. Jurors praised the film’s use of watercolor animation, personal stories, and community broadcasts to explore the importance of a seemingly unimportant little fish.

Director Marco Tribelhorn’s 75 seconds won the Creative Excellence Award. The film documents free-skier Jacob Wester’s 75-second-long battle for survival while trapped in an avalanche.

“A compelling visual treatment brings to life an experience that is impossible to imagine,” said Kerry Stauffer.

Last but not least, the Audience Choice Award went to Only Music // Snake River. Director Justin Fountain’s documentary follows a band that ditches the tour bus for a journey down the Snake River, seeking connection with strangers.

Tickets for the international film festival are now available. We have also covered the recently announced Banff Mountain Book Award winners.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.
