#8346. A Climbing Expedition to 'Central Asia's Patagonia'

A mix of nine young and veteran Croatian climbers spent the month of August in one of the world’s great big-wall destinations — Kyrgyzstan’s Karavshin Range in the Pamir-Alay Mountains. Reaching up to 5,509m, the once-unknown granite cliffs now draw dozens of international climbers each summer. The two core valleys, Ak-Su and Kara-Su, have become known as Central Asia’s Patagonia.

The two leaders, Sinisa Skalec and Lukas Grbac Lackovic, spoke to ExplorersWeb about the place and their expedition. First, a summary of their four weeks.

Though suffering from stomach issues for most of the month (more on this later), the team repeated several granite classics, did a new route on 4,430m Pik Breeze, and attempted another new line.

Three climbers — Bruno Crljenic, Eva Pejic, and Lackovic — identified a potential new line below Pik Breeze’s west ridge. They knew of only one previous route on the mountain, done by Russians Marina Popova and Denis Prokofiev.

Beginning at 5am, the three crossed a broad scree slope, then began climbing a couloir below Pik Breeze’s west ridge. They negotiated loose rock, briefly followed an existing line, then moved left into crack systems.

Canned Horse, 6c A0, top. Below, previous attempts and possible other lines on the face.

They climbed until 9pm, when they bivouacked on a tiny ledge with space for only two people sitting and one lying down. Resuming at 8:30am, they reached a col after three pitches and continued directly up slabs and cracks. They finished on easier IV/V terrain and followed a 200m ridge to the summit.

They called their new line Canned Horse, 930m, 23 pitches, 6c A0. It took them a further eight hours to get back to base camp. During the climb, they found pitons on two pitches. They believe these belonged to another route on the west ridge, but the history of that line, and whether it was finished, is uncertain.

Pejic, Grbac Lackovic, and Crljenic on top of Pik Breeze.

Later in the month, one of those climbers, Eva Pejic, became quite ill and couldn’t leave base camp on her own. Five teammates helped evacuate her, carrying her for 11km, with assistance from other climbers in the area. She later recuperated in the Kyrgyz city of Osh.

On the first crux of ‘Perestroika Crack.’

Three small teams of Croatian climbers also repeated Perestroika Crack, one of the best-known long granite routes in Central Asia. It rises about 900m up the north face of 4,240m Pik Slesova and is graded 7a. Three members reportedly free-climbed the route. One pair, Sinisa Skalec and Ivan Vitez, completed the line in a single marathon day, beginning at 5:45am, summiting at 5:40pm, and returning to base camp at 11pm.

Skalec and Vitez also repeated the French Ridge of 4,510m Kyrkchilta, 2,000m, 6b to 6b+. They needed one bivouac. Toward the end of August, this same pair attempted a first ascent on the Central Pyramid of Ortotyubek, overlooking base camp. Their attempted route mounted slabs and cracks to the left of the established Take It Easy line, which other Croatians had repeated during that month. The pair gave up their attempt on August 26 because of illness.

Base camp with Ortotyubek and Slesova in the background.

Here is our interview with Lukas Grbac Lackovic and Sinisa Skalec about Kyrgyzstan and the Ak-Su Valley.

Lukas Grbac Lackovic repeats the Central Route on a peak called Small Russian Tower.

Was Eva Pejic’s health problem related to altitude or something else? What was the illness you experienced on Ortotyubek?

Eva’s health problem was unrelated to altitude exposure or the expedition environment. However, most of us experienced gastrointestinal infections, with symptoms including diarrhea, bloating, fever, and general malaise.

This significantly hindered our climbing, as everyone in the team got sick at least once, and some even twice. Recovery was slow and never quite complete –- we felt weak for most of the trip.

One thing that helped us recover was having plain rice with some of our meals, which we could get from the cook if we asked nicely. If we were to go again, we would definitely bring our own food and cooking equipment. This can be inconvenient, but it could potentially save you at least a week of being sick and unable to climb.

Another view of base camp and peaks Ortotyubek and Slesova.

Can you describe the Karavshin Valley? How large is it? Are there many side valleys?

The two main valleys are Ak-Su and Kara-Su. Adjacent to them is the Jaupaya Valley, which provides a descent route from Pik Breeze and is home to several peaks around 5,000m high. The rock quality there appears poor, and we are not aware of any established rock routes in the valley.

Another interesting area, west of Kara Su, is the Orto-Chashma Gorge. An Italian team made a first ascent there during our visit.

Here in the West, we first heard about the area following the kidnapping of four American climbers in Kara-Su in 2000. Since then, it seems to have calmed down and become a popular climbing destination. Is the area considered safe nowadays, or are there still things climbers should be aware of?

Currently, the security situation in the valley is good. There is always the possibility of tensions, of course, as the valleys lie close to the border with Tajikistan. However, in recent years, the number of climbers visiting the area has increased exponentially, reaching around 100 climbers this year.

How many other climbing teams were in Ak-Su and neighboring Kara-Su while you were there? Where were they generally from?

During the three weeks we spent in the valley, up to 50 climbers were in BC at the same time. Climbers came from all over the world — Chile, U.S., Spain, Slovakia, Poland, Belgium, Slovenia, France, Italy, Germany, Japan, Russia…

We had originally planned a different expedition, but had to change our destination just a few months before departure. Some friends from Croatia and Slovenia recommended the area because of its straightforward logistics and relatively good security, while still offering impressive big walls and proper crack climbing. It didn’t take much to convince us!

Are there any military checkpoints? Do you need special permission to enter the valleys?

Yes, you have to pass through a military checkpoint when entering the area. However, the outfitter handles the application for the necessary permits and ensures everything goes smoothly.

Some call the Karavshin Valley the Patagonia of Central Asia because of its aesthetic granite walls. Do you think that’s a fair description?

Patagonia is definitely more spectacular, but you pay the price with poor weather. If your goal is to climb almost every day, then the Karavshin Valley is a better choice.

Climbing in Patagonia requires much more logistical planning and preparation, and the approaches themselves are a whole different story. In the Karavshin Valley, on the other hand, there is no snow or ice during the season we visited (August), at least on the walls we climbed. Some of the walls are just a 15-minute walk from BC, and you can reach them all in approach shoes.

Perestroika Crack and some of the classic routes in the Karavshin region are arguably just as good as the best rock routes in Patagonia.

How does the granite compare to Yosemite and Patagonia?

The granite is more similar in texture to that found in Patagonia; it is not as smooth as in Yosemite. The overall rock quality is generally good, although, as everywhere, some sections are of poorer quality. One downside of the mild climate in the Karavshin Valley is the dirt and grass that accumulate in the cracks, which can sometimes detract from the aesthetic appeal of the routes. The more popular and frequently climbed routes have already been cleaned and are pleasant to climb.

You climbed 4-1/2 pitches of the route on Ortotyubek. How much did you have left to complete it?

There were still two-and-a-half pitches left to complete the route up to the junction with Take It Easy. We envisioned the second half of the fifth pitch following the line up to a point just below the overhang that runs diagonally to the left.

From there, the sixth pitch follows a thin crack, from which you need to traverse left and move into another system of thin cracks that runs across the roof and joins the Take It Easy belay (seventh pitch). The last pitch of Take It Easy offers a logical exit, and we don’t think there is any need to come up with an alternative.

At the point where we stopped (first half of the fifth pitch), we left two pitons that we used as a temporary rappel anchor. Repeating parties are welcome to remove them, as they were only left for temporary use. We think a few bolts may be needed in the second half of the fifth pitch, but that obviously depends on the abilities of those planning to complete the route.

All belays are equipped (the first and fourth belays have two 8mm bolts each, while the second and third have two pitons each). In addition, we left two or three pitons in the first pitch and two in the second pitch.

As in the rest of the valley, some sections of the route are filled with dirt and grass. We made an effort to clean the cracks in the first four pitches, but there is still plenty of work to be done, especially on the sixth pitch, which we didn’t climb, where a thin crack is completely filled with dirt.

How much unclimbed rock remains? Are the obvious lines already established, or are there still many promising routes?

The most obvious and accessible lines may have already been climbed, but there is still plenty of potential. Anyone willing to put in the effort can establish a new route. The area is vast, and there is still plenty of room for new routes.

Your website mentions that there are guidebooks to certain routes in that area. Where are these available?

Most of the guidebooks we used are available online. We received some Russian guidebooks from climbing friends, although we didn’t use them very much. We’re not sure how easily they can be found online. Climbers from all over the world bring various materials to the camp, so most route topos and information can be obtained there.

What was the most unexpected experience on your expedition — something that might surprise climbers familiar with other big-wall areas?

We were pleasantly surprised by the kindness and honesty of the Kyrgyz people. When traveling to remote parts of the world, many of us tend to expect that someone will try to rip us off and that we will have to haggle over everything, but that was not the case here.

Although some things took longer than expected, everything we had agreed on in advance was respected, and all the logistics for transportation and accommodation worked very well.

As we mentioned earlier, our only complaint concerns the food and hygiene conditions at the camp, which were most likely responsible for the digestive problems experienced by virtually all the climbers staying there.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8342. Avalanche Fatality Warns About Early Ski Conditions In The Alps

Little snow on the mountains doesn’t mean safe conditions. An American off-piste skier died in the Austrian Alps last weekend after he triggered a small slab avalanche. It was far too small to bury him, but it carried him into an open crevasse below.

With the ski season barely underway, this is Europe’s first avalanche victim this season. Last year, 151 people died in avalanches in Europe between October 2025 and June 2026, according to the European Avalanche Warning Service (EAWS), a shockingly high number.

Most victims perished in the Alps: 40 in Italy, 35 in France, 33 in Austria, and 18 in Switzerland. It was the 13th most deadly year in the Alps since record-keeping began. It was also the highest tally ever since the “Winter of Terror” of 1951. Avalanche prevention standards have improved a lot since then, but the number of leisure skiers, climbers, and hikers in the mountains has multiplied as well. (Most of those 1951 victims lived in villages along avalanche paths.)

Last weekend’s accident took place at the Stubai Glacier ski resort. The glacier sections of the Alps typically open for skiing weeks earlier than other mountain resorts, thanks to their higher altitudes and glacial ice, which can provide good October skiing with just a thin layer of fresh snow on top. Stubai’s ski resort opened on October 9 this year.

A map of Stubai Glacier ski resort with the area where the accident happened circled in red. Photo: Stubaier Gletscher website

The skier was on his own, descending in an out-of-bounds area below East Daunkogel Peak at around 11:30am. His tracks on the snow show he eventually abandoned the snow-covered glacier and ventured onto a steeper, rocky area. At some point, he broke a small slab of crusty snow which dragged him some 50 meters into an open bergschrund, where he fatally fell 20 meters down, according to the Lawinen Report.

The accident site, with the skier’s tracks visible,  and the movement of the snow slab — 50m long and 25m wide — marked in pink. Photo: Lawinen Report

Other skiers in the area saw the accident and raised the alarm, but the skier had died by the time they extracted him from the crevasse. The police said the slab was only 10cm to 30cm thick, and the slide was just 25 meters wide. The crevasse was 50 meters below the fracture point.

Avalanche risks are obvious on heavily loaded slopes, but the accident shows that even a thin layer of unstable snow can cause a fatal fall. In this case, the snow had fallen recently on the verglass ice over glacier-polished smooth rocks, and had no time to settle. This is a typical early-season condition in mountain ranges around the world.

The accident occurred at nearly 3,000m of altitude on a shady northeast slope, where the snow hardly transforms and settles during the short days of late fall. To make things worse, the report notes that wind had picked up that morning, creating a weak crust on top of loose, cold, fresh snow, prone to breaking.

The avalanche site on the following day, with most of the terrain covered by freshly drifted snow. Photo: LWD Tirol

The second factor was exposure: the skier might have easily survived the fall if the terrain had been a clean snow slope, but he was on rocky terrain, where a simple fall can result in serious injuries. Most of all, the crevasse below created what the report described as a “terrain trap.”

Fans of backcountry and ski touring are eager to hit the slopes with the first snowflakes, and the scarcely covered terrain may provide a false sense of safety from avalanches. But objective dangers go beyond the big powder avalanches and also include falls on icy slopes. In addition, the glaciers are dry after the summer, and the crevasses are wide open.

After a mild fall, snow is falling heavily in the Alps this week, significantly improving conditions for the region’s ski resorts. Most of them plan to open this weekend. The snow will also offer backcountry opportunities, but conditions will remain highly unstable.

Snowfall up to two meters is expected today in the Alps. Graphic by meteoexploration.com

The avalanche report today by the Tiroler Avalanche Service (LWD Tirol) shows low risk in the Austrian mountains, a remarkable risk (3 out of 5) in the Swiss Alps, and moderate risk (2 out of 5) in France.

The avalanche forecast for today, November 19, in the Alps shows remarkable (level 3-orange) risk, especially in Switzerland. Graphic: LWD Tirol

#8341. Zero In Hagerstown, MD

Today’s post is a brief memo covering my emotions heading into my final 4 day hiking stint. Mostly I am praying I don’t forget anything!

It was difficult to write up the posts from the last two days. The problem was two-fold. Nerves for the upcoming 4 days on trail and ensuring I had thought of everything so I could attempt the 4 state challenge on Sunday. It would be easy to forget a single thing, like my power bank which I rarely take with me. I was also trying to give myself a mental break, but writing the blog posts takes mental energy, so I was battling a catch-22.

The latest weather outlook looks almost too promising to be true. It looks like it will be reaching 60 degrees each of the upcoming 4 days. Overnight lows are hovering around 40 degrees. That would be a miracle given the time of year. Sunday does look cloudy, so catching the sunset at Pen Mar park might fall a bit flat, but if I am successful with my 4 state challenge attempt nothing will be able to dull the ceremony.

Perhaps the only item of note is that I have been unable to restock my whole milk powder, all the stores in the area have run out and there seems to be a supply issue. I am making a plan to replace my usual breakfast and dinner with something else, probably granola bars and beef sticks. Not ideal, but hopefully a minor change that I can negotiate without impacting my 4 state challenge attempt.

I’ll wrap up the post here, its only been two days since my last zero. There isn’t anything to report that you haven’t read at least a few times already.

Hopefully the butterflies in my stomach are more due to the 3 cups of coffee rather than actual nerves that will seep over into my hiking time tomorrow.

#8338. Family Offers $75,000 Reward to Save Climber Missing on Mount Fuji

Today, November 19, the Yamanashi Prefectural Police announced that they had ended the search for Chinese climber Chen Jie, 25, according to the Japanese national newspaper Mainichi Shimbun. Chen disappeared November 8 on the main Yoshida Trail after continuing alone up 3,776m Mount Fuji during its closed season.

His two companions reportedly turned back because of deteriorating weather and inadequate equipment. Chen continued on his own without crampons or sufficient cold-weather clothing. His last video was sent that morning between 2,300 and 2,700m.

His father subsequently traveled to Japan and offered a reward of RMB500,000 ($75,000) for information leading to his recovery alive, or RMB100,000 ($15,000) for recovery of his body.

Ironically, Chen belonged to China’s Blue Sky Rescue Team, a volunteer search-and-rescue organization. His mother told local media that he had participated in rescues in China and had some mountaineering experience, including trekking and climbing in Nepal and Indonesia.

#8335. Climbers Forge New Route on Mount Shiva Despite Limited Acclimatization

Benjamin Bosshard and Philippo Sala will never forget their first experience above 6,000m and the new route they opened with Stefan Siegrist on India’s Bahai Jar, commonly known as Mount Shiva (6,142m).

Expeditions in the Indian Himalaya are usually quiet while they take place, because satellite phones and InReach devices are prohibited. After returning home, however, Stefan Siegrist reported the details, and the team’s cameraman, Christoph Detschmann, posted a video summary of the expedition:

Siegrist had already visited the Chenab River Valley area in 2018 and climbed Shiva with Jonas Schild, but he still had designs on its unclimbed northwest ridge. At the end of September, he, Sala, and Bosshard reached base camp in the Kishtwar massif.

The three Swiss climbers were prepared to endure a long period of bad weather while acclimatizing. To their surprise, a good weather window opened right away. It was too good to let go, despite not being properly acclimatized and Sala and Bosshard’s lack of altitude experience. The window was expected to last just three days. After many “discussions and doubts,” they left base camp on October 3.

Sala had a headache during the first night, but the following morning, they were all totally focused on the climb, and the tension disappeared. “We assumed that we were unlikely to reach the summit on this attempt, and that we would therefore be wasting our only chance before a major storm [brought] the season to an end,” Siegrist explained. “So from then on, the idea was simply to climb higher and acclimatize, without any pressure.”

A moment of the climb, filmed from a drone by Christoph Detschmann

Along the way, they discovered they had left their stove at base camp. No problem in 2026: The expedition photographer used his drone to carry the stove up to them. The use of drones was a hot topic during the recent Piolets d’Or: To what extent are they compatible with alpine-style criteria? While most accepted that drones could scout routes or sections out of view of base camp, many considered using them to carry equipment up a face to be a red line.

On Shiva, Siegrist says the delivery of the missing stove “proved essential to the success of the climb.”

The climbers spent their second night at 5,250m at the base of the northwest ridge. On the following morning, they had to decide whether to continue or retreat. They all felt well, but starting the highly technical ridge was a point of no return. At the same time, Siegrist recalls, they realized they really had a chance to summit. And so on they went.

That night, they stopped at 5,700m in what Siegrist describes as a crucial moment. His partners had not been at that altitude before, and the latest forecast announced a storm the following day. They decided to wait and see what the morning looked like. No one slept at all that night.

The last meters before the summit. Drone image by Christoph Detschmann

The sky was still clear when the team set off again at 4am, so they chose to keep going. “After a while, we finally reached the final slope, just 300m below the summit,” Siegrist wrote. By now, they knew they were going to make it. They reached the top at 7:44am, six days after arriving in Base Camp, and just in time to hurry down as the storm approached.

The climbers descended via the 1985 Japanese route (see topo below), and reached their base camp tents as it started to snow.

#8332. Congratulations to These 2026 Pacific Crest Trail Finishers (Part 9)

The Pacific Crest Trail is one of North America’s most beautiful and iconic thru-hikes, stretching 2,650 miles from the Mexican border all the way to Canada. Every year, thousands of hikers set out on this unforgettable journey, pushing their limits both physically and mentally as they navigate this incredibly diverse terrain.

This year, wildfires devastated many thru-hikers’ plans. In July, the northern section of the PCT from Harts Pass to the Canadian border was closed due to the Ptarmigan Fire. Because of this, many finish photos look different than in years past.

Thru-hiking teaches lessons in perseverance and resilience, and many hikers were able to pivot from a traditional finish and found creative conclusions to their multi-month journey before the northern terminus officially reopened in mid-September. #8331. Two Inexperienced Teens Rescued After 20 Hours on Grossglockner

Two Germans, aged 15 and 18, spent almost 20 hours on Austria’s highest mountain before being evacuated by helicopter on November 15.

Initially, police reported that the pair had reached the 3,798m summit and become lost while descending. The younger climber disputes this, saying they turned back near an alpine hut at 3,454m because his companion felt ill.

Their ordeal began around 11pm on Saturday, November 14. The pair had no previous experience with such a serious mountain but had prepared by reading websites, watching videos, training physically, and asking others for tips. They did carry crampons, an ice axe, a helmet, a rope, a belay device, and extra clothing.

It took them a long time before they decided to turn around. The descent went well at first, but one spot seemed too dangerous to them. To get around it, they followed someone else’s tracks in the snow, but these suddenly disappeared. While looking for a way, they got stuck in deep snow. The older boy called for help at around 6:30pm on November 15, according to Austria’s public broadcaster, ORF.at.

At first, rescuers tried to guide the pair down by phone. More than two hours later, the boys were still stuck, so a helicopter was dispatched. The rescuers found the pair, exhausted, disoriented, and hypothermic, at around 2,800m.

A rescue helicopter brings the teens to safety. Photo: Mountain Rescue KALS

Yesterday, rescue leader Peter Tembler criticized their lack of alpine experience and preparation. He told the German Press Agency that the pair lacked sufficient experience for the terrain and winter conditions. He also criticized their late-night departure.

According to Tembler, a section that normally takes four to five hours took them almost 12. Other climbers reportedly advised them against continuing.

Tembler believes an increasing number of inexperienced people are attempting serious alpine routes, sometimes encouraged by social media accounts that downplay the difficulty. The disagreement has become a public debate about risk-taking, responsibility, and rescue operations.

As Austria’s highest peak, Grossglockner is a magnet for climbers of all stripes and has more than its share of controversy. In January 2025, a man abandoned his less experienced girlfriend high on the mountain. She froze to death, and an Austrian court later convicted him of manslaughter for leaving her behind. This summer, an altercation occurred on Grossglockner in which a guide insulted a passing climber and nearly pushed him off a cliff. At the same time, veteran alpinists are still setting bold new routes here.

According to Der Standard, Grossglockner gets 8,000 to 9,000 ascents each year, with an average of two dozen rescues annually. Between 2005 and 2025, 29 climbers have died on the mountain.

#8327. Weekend Warm-Up: Hurricane Chase

This episode of the storm-chasing TV series Storm Rising is titled “High Plains Insanity.” Produced by National Geographic, it follows Reed Timmer and Mike Theiss as they attempt to navigate and document an increasingly unpredictable storm season.

The storm-chasing segments are filmed like an action movie but, as Timmer makes clear, thrills are not the primary objective. As the climate changes and disrupts weather patterns, tornadoes are no longer limited to traditional seasons and ranges.

“That’s why we need to be on the ground directly in the path of these tornadoes,” Timmer explains, “to record data that no one else is getting.”

Driving into a potential tornado in Montana. Photo: Screenshot

With hurricane and tornado seasons now overlapping, the duo is forced to split up to cover the widest possible area and collect the most data. For the tornadoes, this means deploying a probe into the funnel.

Up north in the High Plains of the U.S. West and Midwest, Timmer and his dog Gizmo narrowly miss storm after storm. Gizmo is better company but is a very poor navigator, while Timmer isn’t much better. He constantly makes wrong turns as he tries to navigate, drive, and prepare equipment. He does take the time to explain the basic weather science behind what he is failing to catch, such as the conditions that created the baseball-sized hail pummeling his windshield.

Timmer and Gizmo drive through the aftermath of a powerful tornado in the Black Hills of North Dakota. Photo: Screenshot

In Florida, Theiss sleeps in his car to maintain a constant vigil along the coast. Our current scientific abilities, he explains, allow us to track the movement of hurricane systems with decent accuracy. But predicting their strength is much more difficult. Systems can suddenly turn deadly or fizzle out with equally short notice. In this case, the terrible-looking storm comes to nothing. He heads back up north to rejoin Timmer and Gizmo.

Gizmo buffeted by fierce winds. Photo: Screenshot

The reunited trio prepares for a final effort, complete with a windshield-replacement sequence. They immediately drive into the gathering storm, passing through a hail core which, of course, destroys the new windshield. The Lord giveth and taketh away, et cetera. Soon they’re racing on a near-empty tank, as the powerful storm threatens to touch down at any moment.

It’s their last chance this season to deploy the probe. When, after a long, tense chase, they actually manage to do it, both erupt into cheers. Gizmo appears unmoved, despite the fact that this ground probe, deployed in a storm of this type, in this area, has never been deployed before and is sure to yield valuable scientific data.

#8323. Same Clothes, New Country- Camino Day 49

I woke up around 6:30 AM, and since breakfast wasn’t until 7:30, I had time to relax with a cup of instant coffee and take in the misty ocean view.

Every morning, we share the exact same joke. One of us asks, “What do you want to do today?” and the answer is always, “Let’s take a long walk.” The follow-up question is, “What should I wear?”—which is equally funny since we only own one set of clothes.

Today was a big milestone: the day we finally catch a ferry out of Spain and cross into Portugal.

After a great buffet breakfast at the hotel, we started hiking at 8:30 AM. The fog was still rolling in off the water, leaving the ocean barely visible as waves crashed against the rocks below us. Through the mist ahead, a massive church suddenly appeared—the nearly 900-year-old Monastery of Oia, nestled right along the coast in a picturesque little town.

Most of the morning followed dirt paths running parallel to the main coastal highway, occasionally merging onto a wide, yellow-paved shoulder. Reaching the end of the peninsula, we took a shortcut through a quiet town and arrived at the ferry dock around 12:30 PM.

While waiting, I grabbed a squid sandwich and a café con leche, while Knockerz ordered tea and some rather overcooked chicken fingers. Our ferry reservation wasn’t until 4:00 PM, so we hoped to snag space on an earlier boat.

Soon after our food arrived, a small ferry pulled up—a boat small enough to hold only about six passengers. Fortunately, only two other people were heading across, so we hopped right on.

Once we stepped ashore in Portugal, I checked my phone and realized we had gained an hour in a new time zone. Better yet, we had officially made it to a new country, bringing a whole new culture and language to explore.

Right on the waterfront stood a striking cathedral that felt noticeably different from the churches in Spain. Inside, decorative tiles lined the ceiling alongside carved wood, model boats hung from a side altar, and an intricate retablo resembled a tree growing out of a saint.

Since Portugal is famous for port wine, I stopped at a local bar to try a 10-year-old tawny port. It was sweet with a distinct licorice-syrup flavor. Next, I ordered a local red wine that arrived in a massive glass filled all the way to the brim—for just $1.50. Most places only pour a small portion, so I was shocked by the value.

While chatting with fellow southbound Camino hikers we had met on the boat, I accidentally knocked over and spilled my glass. As we wiped up the mess, the waiter walked right back out and refilled my glass completely free of charge.

Because Knockerz hadn’t eaten her stale chicken fingers on the ferry, she ordered a fresh meal at the table. Our entire bill came out to just €8. Soon, a group of pilgrims from Quebec joined our table for a lively conversation before we set off to find a grocery store and our apartment.

Along the way, we got distracted by an empty, historic church filled with centuries-old artwork. This town is truly stunning.

When we finally tried to locate our apartment, we couldn’t quite find the entrance—and immediately experienced how warm and laid-back Portugal is. Several locals stopped to help us search. One man—who looked like he worked for the local water company—asked for our host’s phone number, called them directly, and stayed with us until we reached the door. When we thanked him in Portuguese, he pointed to the Camino bracelet on his wrist and smiled, “Bom Caminho.” We were completely blown away by the kindness.

After dropping our packs and heading back out for dinner, I noticed the side wall of our apartment actually shared a wall with the back of an old church altar (important detail for tomorrows story!). We picked up a few groceries, sat down in the main square with a couple of glasses of wine, and looked out over the white-capped water separating Spain from Portugal. Even though we thoroughly loved Spain, we couldn’t be happier to finally be in Portugal.

#8319. American Climber Dies at 5,800m After Summiting Mera Peak

Richard Carlos Skidmore, 66, of the United States, was found dead in his tent on Mera Peak on Sunday, November 22, after summiting the 6,476m mountain the previous morning.

According to Solukhumbu district police, he reached the summit at approximately 10am on Saturday, November 21, and descended to the high camp at around 5,800m, where he spent the night.

He was found dead the next morning. Past Mera Peak climbers have suggested on social media that it was unusual to overnight in the high camp after summiting so early in the day.

His body was evacuated by helicopter to Kathmandu for an autopsy. The cause of death remains undetermined. An initial report raised the possibility of altitude-related illness or a cardiac event, but neither has been established medically.

Skidmore previously summited Lobuche Peak in 2024, according to the Everest Chronicle and the Italian Alpine Club’s publication Lo Scarpone. So he had previous Himalayan climbing experience above 6,000m.

The Himalayan Database does include Mera Peak, but its coverage of popular trekking peaks is selective. However, it notes that since 2018, four people have died on Mera Peak, not counting Skidmore.

#8316. New Route on Classic Tien Shan Peak

Three Polish climbers have established a new line on the north face of one of the Tien Shan’s best-known climbing peaks.

Michal Makowski, Robert Paluch, and Rafal Zając took 11 hours to climb their new 850m line on 4,777m Free Korea Peak, in Kyrgyzstan’s northern Tien Shan Mountains. They then spent a further 11 hours returning to base camp.

They named the route The Polish Barber, after pioneer American climber Henry Barber, who soloed the wall in 1976. Their line shares the first 250–300m with Barber’s, Makowski told ExplorersWeb.

“According to the information I have, [the remaining 600-650m] was previously unclimbed and had never been attempted by another team,” said Makowski. “We reached the summit ridge and then began rappelling down from there.

Corona Hut, the team’s base camp. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Free Korea Peak — Pik Svobodnaya Koreya in Russian — was already famous among mountaineers in the former Soviet Union because of its 900m north wall. in 2018, Russian climber Valery Balesin suggested that it was no longer possible to find new lines on this well-known face, although advances in steep ice-climbing techniques have clearly opened new possibilities.

“My friends and I researched Free Korea Peak and realized that part of the north face still has unclimbed terrain,” said Makowski.

On the Polish Barber route. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Their route was almost completely ice climbing. The crux came midway up the wall, when they faced a particularly steep section of ice that they graded at Alpine Ice 4+. The rest of the route was 3/3+. Fortunately, “protection on the Polish Barber was really good, except for the last 150m, where ice was pretty thin and crusty,” said Makowski.

Climbing by moonlight. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Despite its former popularity, they had the valley almost to themselves during their week in camp. There was just one Russian group doing a tourist trek up nearby Corona Peak.

A scrambly section on Free Korea Peak. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8313. Day 40: The Meditation After the Party

I awoke at 9 am to the sound of an alarm clock that wasn’t mine. It was the sound I dreaded to hear.

My Italian brother was getting back on the Camino while my Argentinian brother took a rest day.

I walked my Italian brother out, hugging him multiple times and not wanting to let go. We met on the first day of the Camino, and over the course of the journey, he had become one of the most important people in my life.

Hug after hug after hug, neither of us wanting it to be the last.

The longer the goodbye went on, the more emotional he became.

Tears streamed down his face right before he put on his sunglasses to cover his eyes.

My Argentinian brother and I packed up our backpacks and leisurely left the place we were staying. On the way out, the Venezuelan woman who ran the property told us to come back because “es tu casa”.

We went directly to the cathedral plaza. With our other pilgrim friends, we soaked up the sun knowing it’d be the last day we would see it due to the weather forecast being full of rain over the next few days.

Others were staying, but we all didn’t want to move from that spot and have to say any more goodbyes.

But my Argentinian brother and I had a mission to go on: our meditation in the woods.

Together, we walked forty minutes to a park outside of the city.

There, I was able to process the immensity of emotions and thoughts that Santiago, my birthday, the goodbyes, and thoughts of the future brought.

I realized some powerful things about what was next for my life.

And in all of it, my brother was by my side.

As the afternoon progressed, we kept moving spots to where the sun was. The shade was cold but the sun felt like a warm blanket of comfort.

We watched the clouds slowly swallow the sun. I was getting cold, but my brother was lying on the ground with no desire to leave. In Spanish, he said,

The day together was exactly what we both needed. We were held by nature in a way we couldn’t frequently experience on the Camino.

Every good thing must come to an end. Other pilgrim friends I hadn’t seen in a while made it to Santiago, and it was time to meet them.

My brother and I walked arm in arm through the city to the apartment the dads rented. It took us to the side of the city we hadn’t yet explored, and it was beautiful. Old stone streets, large parks with open spaces, old trees, and views of the city.

The old tree that overlooked the cathedral called to me. Without a care what the locals thought, I ran to the tree and hugged it like it was a long-lost family member.

It was so refreshing to see the dads and catch up as we walked around town to a restaurant for dinner.

Two more pilgrims joined us, and it was a great meal, but I could feel my body shifting and a cold coming on.

We went to the pilgrim pub, Momo, for one drink and then walked back to the cozy apartment to rest much earlier than the night before.

On the comfy couch, my Argentinian brother and I watched videos together from the night before.

So much joy filled my heart reflecting on the past 48 hours.

#8311. Ukrainian Solos New Route in Gokyo Valley, Near Everest

Mykyta Balabanov of Ukraine has soloed a new route on the northeast face of Nepal’s Phari Lapcha (6,017m). Also known as Machhermo Peak, it sits 24km west of Everest in the Gokyo Valley.

Balabanov is still trekking back to Lukla, but has given ExplorersWeb some details and photos about this impressive new route.

The Ukrainian set off on his own up an unclimbed line on the northeast face on November 19. He made two bivouacs on the way up and one more on the descent.

“The level of difficulty is hard to tell, as always when solo, but I’d say it was around 80° on ice, M5+, 5c on rock, and lots of poor snow!” Balavanov said.

He estimates the new route is 1,200m long, and spans some 850 vertical meters from base to summit.

Rough topo of Balabanov’s solo route on the northeast face of Phari Lapcha. Photo: Mykyta Balabanov

Another side of Phari Lapcha saw action just one day before Balabanov, when U.S. guide Bennie Lieber led Canadian Emma Deguara up the Stone(d) Yeti route on the south face.

“I was not related to that climb, as I climbed alone after approaching from a different valley,” Balabanov explained. “However, I saw their tracks on the summit and spotted their bivy place at the bottom of the south face.”

Lieber and Deguara made the second ascent of their chosen route. “While the technical grade (M3, AI2+) is easily digestible, the room for mistakes, due to exposure, is zero,” Lieber said.

Mykyta Balabanov and fellow Ukrainian Mikhail Fomino opened a new route on Ama Dablam in 2024, but they first became widely known for their new route on the Southeast Ridge of Annapurna III in 2021.

#8305. What Happens When You Come Home From Your Thru-Hike?

This guest post was written by Amy “Yardsale” Johnson. Full bio at the end.

There are endless articles, Youtube videos, books, and discussions about preparing for trail. But what happens when we come back home?

This question receives far less guidance. But for many hikers, “coming home” is one of the hardest parts.

I thru-hiked the Appalachian Trail and hiked 1600 miles of the Continental Divide Trail, and I have felt the challenge of spending months on trail and then coming home to a “society” that feels so at odds with the trail experience.

How might we be able to carry the gifts of the trail with us and create a life and a world that is more deeply alive?

For months, we sleep beneath the stars, watch the Milky Way move across the sky, catch the sunrise, and feel the cool breeze of dawn, brimming over with possibility as birds greet the day. As we walk, carrying everything we need on our backs, demands and expectations fall away.

There is no one left to impress, and no one left to be, other than our raw, unfiltered, beautiful selves. In this rawness and vulnerability, we meet each other. We walk, we talk, we sleep under the stars. We bear witness to one another’s stories, we hold one another up. We watch as the first flowers open in the spring, and then as the first leaves turn crimson, set on fire in the fall.

Somewhere along the way, we find deep connection with ourselves, with others, and with nature. We find more space, more color, more life. For the first time, perhaps, we have eyes to see.

We return to our jobs and routines, and the demands and expectations return too. Suddenly, how people interact with us is dependent on how we look, how much money we make, or where we’re from.

We’re expected to work, probably a lot of hours, cooped up indoors, often without a break. With little appreciation, perhaps little financial reward, and little connection to how our work might benefit people or nature. The core of who we are, which had emerged so quietly on trail, sometimes feels buried once again.

I believe the connection and community that we experience on trail open up an incredible possibility. In returning home, we have a chance to carry these gifts with us. We have a chance to create a kinder and more beautiful world: a world with more space, more color, and more life. I believe we can start by rewriting our own lives, step by step, and then by participating in the rewriting of our own small corner of the world. And maybe, this journey is the most important trail of all.

We can begin by considering how we can incorporate small aspects of our experience into our everyday life (and then more, and more, until our lives are more and more aligned).

After all, it’s one step after another that allowed us to hike for hundreds, or thousands, of miles. Are our everyday lives really so different from the trail? Isn’t the natural world all around us? The birds, the flowers, the trees, right outside our windows? The people around us every day, are they not people just like people on trail?

And if people on trail can form supportive, inclusive, beautiful communities, why shouldn’t we also be able to do that in our daily lives?

We all have the power to act, to respond, to create something in this world. On trail, we persevere in the face of conditions that we have no control over: difficult weather, trail conditions, and the physical limitations of our body. Truly, we are small and weak in the face of the power and beauty of Mother Nature.

But we have the power to respond, participate, and persevere. And as it turns out, that power ends up taking us pretty far. From Georgia to Maine, or Mexico to Canada, or any distance that we so choose.

Similarily, in society, we are all small and weak in the face of many of the powerful forces around us. Our culture, the economy, the govermment, and the people around us are all forces that we have little control over. But just like on trail, we always have the power to respond, to participate, and to persevere. And I believe that power can take us pretty far. To a better world, I venture to imagine.

I think we can get there by leaning into the same things that we leaned into on trail: community, perseverance, connection with nature, trust in ourselves, and other people.

Before hiking, I never thought I could hitchhike, that I could navigate remote wilderness on my own, or that I could literally walk from Georgia to Maine. I never imagined that I could break so many “rules” of our society. But I did, and what I found through that vulnerability was deep connection, deep love, faith, and an abiding confidence.

Might it be that we are playing by unspoken rules in our everyday lives that might be holding us back from true confidence, true connection, and new possibilities?

What might happen if we start to question them, and connect anyway?

When we have an urgent need on trail, oftentimes we have no choice but to ask for help. So like it or not, we swallow our pride, get over our fears, and ask some stranger if they can help. Usually, they do, and gladly.

What might happen if we began to do that in our everyday lives? What might happen if we offered that same level of support to others?

What new possibilities might break open, once we begin to realize that the trail was never separate from society, nor was the end of our hike the end of our journey.

What might the next steps look like in practice?

Let’s start with a few questions to consider. Everyone has their own unique experience on trail, and chances are, no two hikers’ “return-home journeys” will be the same. Journaling is one tool that I’ve found helpful in processing my experiences.

What did you experience on trail? How did you feel, and what new perspectives did you find? What aspects of your hike contributed to feeling the way you did?

What perspectives or ways of being did you find on trail that you want to hold onto? What are some small steps you can take to incorporate those perspectives or ways of being into your daily life?

Additionally, below are some of the small practical changes that I’ve found helpful. Have your own practices? Add them to the conversation.

1. Spend the first half hour or more of the day doing something just for you: outside and active is even better. This can set the tone for the entire day. The day belongs to you, to nature, to life itself. Everything else fits into that deeper truth.

4. Get outside regularly. It could be day hikes, weekend backpacking trips, or just taking in the sunlight for 5 minutes from your porch.

5. Along the lines of getting outside: connect with God, the universe, nature, or a sense of meaning beyond yourself.

6. Stay in touch with trail friends, and find like-minded people at home. Going on the post-trail journey with people “who get it” is immeasurably valuable.

7. Find ways to have the authentic relationships like the ones you found on trail. How might you be able to give and receive help more freely, and be yourself without reserve, with the people closest to you?

8. Find joy in the small things. Maybe it’s a fresh bouquet of flowers from the farmers market, the sunlight streaming in through the window, or the birds chirping.

9. Think about how your work can contribute to others, to yourself, and to nature. Start to think about how you might be able to negotiate your work, or your relationship with work, to find better work-life balance, and/or a deeper sense of meaning and contribution. It doesn’t all have to be better all at once. Small steps in the right direction is all that’s needed.

10. Learn about money. Things like budgeting, the time value of money, how debt works, saving and investing. Like it or not, our world runs on money. Money is one of the major keys to more freedom in our world. Use it as a tool to create more space, joy, abundance, and freedom for yourself, your community, and nature.

11. Acceptance. Accepting things we can’t control allows us to have more peace. And we can lean into that which we can control: our response, which is powerful. Just like on trail.

Amy “Yardsale” Johnson is a long-distance hiker, writer, speaker, retreat host, and founder of the Blue Compass Institute. She creates writing and experiences that break open possibilities so that people can create more deeply thriving and connected lives. Her work can be found at https://www.bluecompassinstitute.com, or follow along on Instagram @bluecompassinstitute.

#8302. High Winds Thwart Latest Everest Attempt

The Russian team has returned to Everest, aiming to reach at least 7,500m, but high winds have again dashed their plans.

The three-man team of Andrei Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov set off yesterday and reached their cached tent at 5,600m. As leader Vasiliev explained, they expected strong winds at summit altitude until Wednesday, but they intended to keep themselves in lower areas, where the wind speed was acceptable.

However, the wind was too strong to go higher than 5,600m today, so they decided to stay put. MountainClub.com, which has access to the team’s InReach tracker, has posted a partial view of the climbers’ current location:

We asked Jost Kobusch of Germany (also planning to climb the West Ridge of Everest this winter) about that location. He confirmed that they are midway up the vertical mixed section leading from the Khumbu Glacier to the Lho La Pass.

This is the expedition’s fourth foray up the mountain. According to Vasiliev, it is also their last chance to acclimatize before a potential summit push. The Russians’ climbing permit is reportedly valid until December 15.

Their future progress will depend on the wind. Multi-model forecasts indicate that winds are expected to decrease on Thursday but not to disappear. The chart by High Asia Avalanche Watch, which compares seven different weather models, predicts that winds will not drop below 45kph at the summit (on Thursday and Friday). They will then pick up again.

Wind speed at the summit of Everest over the next two weeks, by High Asia Avalanche Watch.

#8299. German Women Climb New Route on Sharphu VI

Last month, five women with the German Alpine Club established a new route on the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI in eastern Nepal. It was only the second overall ascent of the 6,076m peak near Kangchenjunga.

In October, Steffi Feistl, Anna Gomeringer, Kristin Hinkelmann, Kathi Huber, and Fenja Kochl set up their base camp at 4,000m. A huge amount of snow had fallen earlier that season, guaranteeing arduous slogging in some areas.

Their preliminary plan had been to attempt either Sharphu II or Sharphu VI, but Sharphu II’s condition looked too dangerous due to unstable rock, threatening seracs, and hazardous snow conditions.

Sharphu II and VI. Photo: Xtreme Climbers/labels by Gunter Seyfferth

On the other hand, the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI seemed feasible. The team acclimatized over several reconnaissance trips, overnighting up to 4,800m. They then set up their advanced base camp on a moraine ridge at that altitude. Then Kochl, Hinkelmann, and Huber set off over initially easy but deep snow-covered terrain.

They eventually reached a steep section at around 5,300m where powder snow overlying bare rock made placing protection difficult. “The mixed pitches had looked easy, but were steeper than expected,” recalled Huber. “Nasty, granular snow, and no hold on the rock underneath.”

Their work complete, the trio rappelled down, and the following day, Feistl and Gomeringer took their turn. They inched up a difficult mixed pitch followed by 200 vertical meters of burrowing through waist-deep powder.

The route up the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

After a rest day, the five women set off for the summit. They reached a glacier plateau via a 5,800m, previously unclimbed sub-peak. Here, they bivvied. On October 21, they began the final two kilometers to the main summit. The recent sun had hardened the snow, so they sank at most knee-deep. Eventually, they reached the summit ridge. They had to take turns standing on the summit, because that final ridge was so narrow.

Gomeringer and Kochl on the summit of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

Sharphu VI was first climbed on March 25, 2025, by a Chilean-Nepalese team who thought they’d climbed a different mountain. They went up via the east flank. Before that, Sharphu VI had been attempted only once, in 2023.

The German women’s route featured 1,300m of elevation gain, graded M5, 55°. The expedition was the culmination of the German Alpine Club’s three-year women’s training program.

#8296. A Man Just Ran 3,015 Miles From San Francisco to New York in 41 Days, Setting New Record

When Cameron Balser left San Francisco at 4:59 a.m. on October 10, he set out to run the 3,015-mile Trans America route across the United States in under 42 days in pursuit of a new transcontinental record. About six weeks later, he reached New York City Hall in lower Manhattan at 11:45 p.m. on Friday, November 20, finishing in 41 days, 16 hours, 46 minutes, and 45 seconds.

His crossing beat the previous transcontinental record by more than seven hours and cleared the 42-day mark with just over seven hours to spare. For the final stretch down Manhattan’s West Side Drive, a large group of supporters ran alongside him to the finish.

The final push in New York. Photo via Bernie Bernstein

Data collected during the run shows how Balser broke the record due to consistency. Over the 42 days, he averaged 72.1 miles per day with a median pace of 74.5 miles per day. He hit at least 70 miles on 33 of his 42 days and crossed the 75-mile threshold 18 times.

On his third day, Balser logged his biggest climb of the entire crossing, gaining 14,210 feet of elevation over 69 miles. By the time he reached Manhattan, he had accumulated 148,546 feet of total vertical gain. Balser ran 500 miles in 6.4 days, 1,000 miles in 13 days, and 2,000 miles in 27.2 days.

Balser’s only notable slowdown came in early November, when he recorded his shortest day of the crossing at 42.2 miles in 10 hours and 10 minutes, followed by 45.5 miles the next day and 59.4 miles on November 9. However, by November 12, he had returned to a 75-mile day. In the following week, which would mark the end of his attempt, Balser covered 542.5 miles, averaging 77.5 miles per day. By comparison, his opening week totaled 504.7 miles at a 72.1-mile daily average.

Cameron Balser in New York City. Photo via Bernie Bernstein

His final day into New York City was the biggest push of the entire run. On that day, Balser covered 91.8 miles in 18 hours and 48 minutes, which also was his only day surpassing 80 miles.

At the end of the run, Balser focused on the motivation behind the miles rather than the clock.

“It was very humbling in a lot of ways. I wanted people to know how beautiful they already are and how loved they are,” Balser said. “Getting that message out by running 75+ miles a day is more important than the record. That’s why I did this, to show people how beautiful they are.”

Looking at the crowd that turned out for his late-night arrival in New York, he added: “I did not expect that many people to come out, it was a surreal moment… I went back to the memories of the people who helped us. I just really appreciated everyone’s help.”

#8294. Piolet d'Or Climber Bags New Route on Kangchung Shar

Hiroyoshi Manome of Japan and his partners, Koji Shiogai and Subaru Takeda, are back in base camp after climbing a new route via the southwest pillar on Kangchung Shar in the Everest region.

The three climbed the line in four days and three nights from their base in Gokyo, Manome’s home team told ExplorersWeb. They summited in good weather and clear skies yesterday at noon. “The route was particularly interesting, with substantial technical rock climbing,” they explained.

The team believes it is the first ascent of this particular line and, as far as we know, they are right. The Himalayan Database has no record of any previous ascent of the southwest pillar.

Manome during the climb. Photo: Subaru Takeda

Kangchung Shar, located in the Gokyo Valley, has an altitude that varies between 6,030m to 6,103m, depending on the source. The Japanese team has shown that the peak still has plenty of adventurous terrain.

Manome explained before departure that their primary goal was the formidable 1,000m mixed north face, which has been climbed only once, with the east ridge as a backup.

“It will be a technical climb, which suits me just right, and there is little information available, so I think it will be an adventurous climb,” he said. The climber illustrated his plans with the hard-drawn sketch below. However, in the end, the climbers found a third option.

The Japanese team’s two potential routes. Photo: Hiroyoshi Manome

Kangchung Shar was first climbed in 1984 by Franci Knez of Slovenia, on a solo up the northwest ridge from Kangchung La. The 1,000m north face of Kangchung Shar was first climbed by  Zdenek Hak and Jaroslaw Bansky of the Czech Republic on their third try in 2021. No one has repeated it. After summiting, the Czech pair traversed to the south side for their descent.

Another Japanese team climbed neighboring Kangchung Nup (6,090m) in the spring of 2022. The American Alpine Journal describes Kangchung Shar and Kangshung Nup as “a bit like two Matterhorns side by side but two kilometers higher.”

An alpinist for 30 years, Hiroyoshi Manome focuses on technical, alpine-style climbs of 6,000m Himalayan peaks. He received a Piolet d’Or for the first ascent of the 2,200m south pillar of Kyashar (6,770m) in Nepal, with Tatsuya Aoki and Yasuhiro Hanatani. His latest big climb was another first ascent in November 2024, this time of the northeast ridge of Nepal’s Chhopa Bamare (6,109m), with Akitugu Konishi and Kotaro Kunimune.

Hiroyoshi Manome. Photo: The North Face Japan

#8291. November: A Dangerous Time of Year in the Mountains

A glut of early-winter mountain accidents around the world suggests that this transition season may be the most dangerous time of year. It’s not too cold, and many places don’t have enough snow to impede hiking. It’s tempting to let our guard down.

Just north of Los Angeles this week, an experienced hiker and trail runner died when he slipped on an icy slope while trying to help his son.

Guillermo Batres, 44, was hiking on 2,865m Mount Baden-Powell in California’s San Gabriel Mountains with his 14-year-old son, Sebastian. Sebastian slipped on the icy trail but managed to grab a tree. Batres rushed to help him but slipped himself, falling 150m down a ravine.

Batres, in green, and his son seconds before the fatal slip. Photo: Screenshot

Two passing hikers gave the teen microspikes and helped him to safety. A sheriff’s helicopter lowered a paramedic into the ravine, but Batres was already dead.

Also in California, in Angeles National Forest, Nathan Sakaue, 14, died after falling 150m into an icy ravine. A second teenager also fell but survived with injuries. A third member of the party remained above and summoned help.

Search and Rescue services in the Angeles National Forest have responded to six incidents over the last seven days.

Angeles National Forest, California. Photo: Shutterstock

Elsewhere, a 31-year-old man from the Denver area reached the summit of Colorado’s Humboldt Peak (4,288m) on Sunday morning before suffering a fatal fall.

Winter conditions hampered the search, which lasted two days. Colorado Army National Guard personnel located his body yesterday afternoon.

In Banff National Park, Canada, two skaters fell through thin ice at Lake Louise and were able to clamber out, while a day later, a third skater fell through and died of hypothermia. Skating on “wild ice” is a popular sport in the Canadian Rockies at this time of year, but temperatures fluctuate day to day, and ice that is solid enough today may quickly thin within hours if temperatures rise above freezing.

Early-season skating on Lake Louise in Banff National Park can be magical, as long as the ice is thick enough. Photo: Jerry Kobalenko

In France, a 22-year-old solo hiker was found dead beneath snow following a 24-hour search by Chamonix mountain gendarmes.

Following recent heavy snowfall, avalanche danger in the Mont Blanc massif reached Level 4 out of 5 above 2,200m. Authorities had urged people to reconsider mountain outings, but whether an avalanche caused his death has not been established.

Also in France this week, three separate parties needed rescue when wintry weather set in. On Mont Valier in the Pyrenees, three trail runners poorly equipped for snow needed helicopter evacuation. Elsewhere in the Pyrenees, two inexperienced hikers became stranded in snow and needed a helicopter rescue. And in Isère in the French Alps, four young hikers struggled to descend in deteriorating weather and snow. Rescuers accompanied them to safety.

Italy’s Apennine Mountains. Photo: Shutterstock

A 56-year-old man slipped on snow at around 2,500m between Corno Grande and Corno Piccolo in the Apennine Mountains of central Italy. He suffered serious injuries. His wife administered first aid and called rescuers. As darkness descended, an Italian Air Force helicopter capable of night operations was brought in and eventually airlifted the man to a hospital.

Finally, in Japan’s Ōmine Mountains southeast of Osaka, a mountaineer in his 40s became lost and stranded in the snow and needed rescue.

Accidents happen in mountains throughout the year, but late November is particularly perilous. At times it can feel almost summery, and then without warning, winter hits.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8288. Sick, Tired Climbers Bail on Everest

At last word, the Russian team attempting Everest’s West Ridge was waiting out high winds in their tent at 5,600m. Twenty-four hours later, the team has revealed their expedition is over.

“We’re back at the lodge; we just don’t have the strength left,” leader Andrei Vasiliev told MountainClub today from Lobuche. He explained that the entire team was suffering from colds and coughs that had accompanied them on their previous trips up the mountain. In addition, Vitaly Shipilov has an injured shoulder.

“We might have been able to push through this current outing, but we objectively wouldn’t have enough left for another push,” Vasiliev went on.

This most recent push was the last acclimatization round the team needed before considering a final summit climb. They intended to reach 7,500m and then rest before the final effort. However, the climbers are exhausted and ill, and the conditions on the mountain are no better. Vasiliev noted the seracs are falling more frequently than they were at the start.

“We’ve decided not to take any unnecessary risks,” he explained.

The team will now focus on a new attempt next spring. “I’m channeling my frustration and dissatisfaction as fuel for a future attempt,” he concluded.

#8286. Trail Marshall vs. Troll Marshal, Gifts for Hikers, and Who’s a Hiker Bro | This Week on Trail #3

Trail Marshall has 88,000 Facebook followers, a bio promising love and positivity, and calls the Appalachian Trail a “Bourbon Street in the woods.”

On this episode of This Week on Trail, produced by The Trek, hosts Jess “Sassafras” and Katie “Double Dip” try to decide whether he’s a whistleblower or a professional rage-baiter, and what to make of the Facebook parody group built to mock him. Plus, gift picks from The Trek’s holiday guides, a survey comment section demanding justice for SOBOs, a Hiker Bro Quiz showdown, and a listener tip about a beloved New York shelter that the hosts couldn’t confirm.

Trail Marshall is one of the most-followed thru-hiking personalities on Facebook, with a bio listing the Mountains-to-Sea Trail, the Benton MacKaye, and AT hikes in 2020, 2021, 2023, and 2025. Jess, who brought the story, describes him as someone some hikers call infamous and others call a whistleblower, and notes he seems to enjoy being controversial. Katie says she’s not in the whistleblower camp, but she can’t open Facebook without reading his latest rant all the way through.

Jess walks through his “Toxic Truths of the Appalachian Trail” series, which he says will become a book (both hosts plan to buy it). In installments like “AT Scam Culture,” Trail Marshall alleges that most hostels and shuttles are grifters or opportunists, criticizes some for-profit trail angels, and regularly calls out the ATC and ALDHA. He also describes the trail south of New Jersey as a “Bourbon Street in the woods” and alleges an organized drug network that protects dangerous people, even recasting the community’s practice of circulating photos of men who make women uncomfortable as a way of punishing drug mules. Katie grants that the AT likely sees more crime than other trails because it runs so close to population centers, and the hosts are openly skeptical of the drug-mule theory.

Then there are the haters. Jess explains that critics built a Facebook group called the Troll Marshal Community, posting AI parody comics of his likeness and thinly veiled jabs dressed up as trail etiquette tips. The group has since been taken down, and Trail Marshall says it was removed because it was illegal harassment, calling it cyberbullying and cyberstalking and alleging that hiking organizations shared it. Jess says she saw no evidence of that last claim, and that mocking a real person this way is a messed-up thing to do. Along the way, there’s a story about someone Venmo-ing him pennies just to send mean messages, and a debate over what it would cost to insult each host.

Neither host can tell how much Trail Marshall believes and how much is engagement strategy. Katie notices that his most enthusiastic commenters rarely seem to be thru-hikers saying his claims match their own experience, and Jess points to a recent run of posts about missing hikers and possible serial killers on the AT, some involving crimes with no clear tie to the trail, that she calls borderline fearmongering. Both agree there’s a real conversation to have about crime and situational awareness on trail, just not one that tells people the AT is too dangerous to hike. Jess also relives the night her own face showed up on his page, and the hosts want to hear what listeners think.

Trek writers and Bloggers weighed in on this year’s gift guides, and each host picks two favorites. Jess starts with Superfeet insoles ($50), a stocking stuffer for hikers with foot pain that she suggests gifting with a receipt, since the right model depends on the foot, and follows with the Kindle Paperwhite, the splurge she packs for reading at camp because her eyes tire on a phone screen. Katie picks Fillo’s Walking Tamales ($35 for seven), a shelf-stable snack both hosts have been eating, though Katie wishes it packed more protein (a flavor taste test is apparently in the works). Her other pick is the $12 Chicken Tramper Ultralight Gear Bear Can Key, which she started using early in her CDT hike and calls a rare true game changer for the price.

A Trek video recapping stats from the 2025 AT Thru-Hiker Survey drew comments less about the data than about the deadline. Jess, who works with the survey data, agrees that closing it in early November shortchanges SOBOs and flip-floppers still on trail, and announces an extended deadline. She also agrees that 279 responses is a small sample and wants many more hikers to take part, while both hosts push back on a commenter who says section hikers are ignored: the survey is designed to help future thru-hikers plan, though long-section hikers can still contribute.

In a new segment, the hosts take on a Spotify comment about the crying-baby-at-a-campground episode that called out “two childless women” for joking about suffocating babies. They concede the “childless women” part is factually accurate, acknowledge that the jokes won’t land for everyone, and apologize, then offer the commenter one free shot at their own insecurities, which detours into a cat-flea saga.

A Backpacker Radio clip has Zach taking the Hiker Bro Quiz from Eliza “Swerve” Sweeney, landing at 45% bro after admitting he eats coffee powder with no water. Katie scored 60%, officially a “certified hiker bro,” though she’d rather call herself “a bro in good standing.”

Listener Jimbo writes in that Ralph’s Peak Hikers’ Cabin, the RPH Shelter in New York and one of the few spots on the AT where you can get a pizza delivered, is slated for demolition in mid-2027. The hosts couldn’t find anything online to confirm it, so if you know more, send it their way.

Katie joins Zach and Andrew Skurka for Backpacker Radio LIVE in Denver on December 5 (ugly holiday sweaters encouraged), and Jess invites anyone planning a long-distance hike in 2027 to apply to blog for The Trek.

Have a story lead, a question, feedback, or something we should discuss? Send us a note through our contact form or email [email protected]. Links to photos of your cat are also welcome.

#8278. Introducing: Nomadic Nurse Goes Hiking

Hey hikers!! My name is Emilie and I am originally from flat land Ontario in Canada, but now I work as a nurse in beautiful British Columbia. I am hiking the Pacific Crest Trail in 2027 which is why I am here, because my friends and family will all be wondering where I am, so it is easier to give updates this way.

“Flat land” Ontario at 1000ft – top of Sleeping Giant

Though I love day hiking, I have never done a long distance thru-hike. I grew up car camping in Ontario and I have recently fallen in love with backpacking through mountains. I have hiked the West Coast Trail in BC and I am going to hike the W-trek in Patagonia in early 2027. I have always loved the outdoors and being active, which is why I have chosen to thru-hike in 2027.

Other than my outdoor hobbies, I am getting more into crafts such as crocheting and painting. One of my goals one day is to get a small watercolour book and paint the scenery of places I have hiked to! I don’t think that is very ultralight though, so perhaps I will save that for my post-PCT trail life.

I am a travel nurse, which means that I constantly move from town to town to work in under-serviced hospitals, so I am never in the same place for longer than a month. I regularly live out of a suitcase, so down-sizing to a backpack shouldn’t be too hard for me! I already know what my essentials are and have learned to not pack my fears into my suitcases.

I choose to work in rural British Columbia, which means I am lucky to see the rugged and less tourist-heavy regions of BC. I am writing this post from North Vancouver Island, which has one of the highest cougar concentrations in the world, though I have yet to see one. I am hoping that won’t be one of the “firsts” I get to experience on the PCT.

Views from rural British Columbia – not a bad place to work

Other than the obvious which is that hiking is better than working, the short version is that I am hiking because I can. Unfortunately, I do see a lot of sick patients in my job, a lot are older however some are young. I have learned from my patients that life is worth living when you are healthy and capable of experiencing it. “Do it while your body allows you” as they always say.

Society tells you that you need to work hard while you are young to enjoy your retirement. I see so many recent retirees dying, and wonder if they truly got to do the things that make them happy in life. On hard trail days, I will think of patients who aren’t able to be out there.  Stay tuned for a future post going more in depth regarding my reasons to hike.

#8275. Senate Passes BAAJA, the Massive Permitting Bill That Will Expedite Development on Public Lands

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 11:58 AM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

A controversial bill aimed at speeding up infrastructure projects on public lands passed the U.S. Senate Thursday night and now heads to the House.

The Senate declined to vote on a flurry of last-minute amendments to the nearly 500-page text of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act — including one that would have banned data centers on public land, before voting for the third time late Thursday night. Support in the Senate eroded over the series of votes, from 73-25 on the first vote Nov. 9 to a final vote of 62-33. A bill requires a simple majority of 51 votes in the Senate to pass.

Related: Senate Poised to Vote on Affordability Package that Hunters, Anglers Worry Is a Trojan Horse for Public-Land Rollbacks

BAAJA is co-sponsored by Sen. Martin Heinrich, a Democrat from New Mexico often considered a conservation champion, and Sen. Mike Lee, the Utah Republican and architect of recent attempts to sell public land. Heinrich says BAAJA cuts red tape and will speed up renewable energy projects to help fight climate change, increase jobs, and bring down energy costs for average Americans.

Conservation advocates, however, fear it will also lock in projects many sportsmen and women have spent years fighting, like a copper-sulfide mine by the Boundary Waters Canoe Area Wilderness or the Pebble Mine in Alaska’s Bristol Bay. It also may well open the door for massive data centers on public land, something most of the conservation and sporting community resoundingly oppose.

“I understand this is a big, bipartisan bill — it has some things in it I would rather it not have. But I negotiated the deal based on what was actually possible to get signed into law,” Heinrich wrote in an email to Outdoor Life earlier this week.

BAAJA proponents say it increases project certainty. Right now, one administration can reverse or cancel permits approved by a prior administration. But by limiting the back and forth between administrations the bill also drastically changes how much oversight a project can receive, even under foundational laws like the Endangered Species Act, Clean Water Act and National Historic Preservation Act. The bill will also limit public input into projects and judicial overview.

Many of the nation’s tribes have forcefully spoken out against the bill saying it severely curtails tribal input.

“Overall, the bill, by weakening these bedrock laws, would make it far easier to fast-track AI data centers as well as fossil fuel, mining, and other extractive infrastructure. Once these projects are approved, they would be difficult to unwind,” according to the Western Environmental Law Center. “BAAJA would cause grave, generational harm to the West’s public lands, waters and wildlife, and communities.”

Heinrich has said he opposes data centers on public land.

“To be clear, BAAJA is federal legislation focused on energy infrastructure and grid connectivity,” a spokesperson told OL in an emailed satement. “It does not ‘permit’ data centers on public lands. State and local government approval is still required. The Senator’s legislation addresses the federal energy infrastructure challenges associated with a grid that cannot keep up with demand; it does not circumvent the state and local processes that determine whether specific projects move forward.”

Even conservation groups who support portions of the bill question how fast such a massive piece of legislation is moving through Congress. This is the first major permitting reform bill in decades and was introduced Sept. 30.

Read Next: Public-Land Sales, Energy Dominance, and Data Centers: What Interior’s Master Plan Really Means for Outdoorsmen

The bill now heads to the House. If representatives make any changes to the Senate version, the Senate must vote again on the bill before it heads to President Trump’s desk for his signature. The president has said he supports the bill.

This story was updated on Nov. 20 at 2:30 p.m. CST to include comment from Sen. Heinrich’s office.

Christine Peterson is a freelance writer based in southeast Wyoming covering hunting, fishing, outdoor recreation, wildlife and the environment for Outdoor Life, High Country News, National Geographic and others.
